## A.21 Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1-3 pages)
The objective of this project is to enhance Direct Access (EDA) in Micronesia. The climate change adaptation solutions for the Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia is to shift the status quo from a pathway of climate vulnerability to one of improved food and water security, enhanced disaster risk reduction (DRR) and increased resilience to climate change. This project will provide technical assistance to the Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) to one of improved food and water security, enhanced disaster risk reduction (DRR) and increased resilience to climate change.

Climate change is expected to severely threaten FSM across all sectors of its economy, particularly through increased sea level rise, variability of rainfall, severe weather events and king tides; and temperature. Past inundation, surge and storm events have caused significant losses and damages to communities' assets and livelihoods, including loss of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 4 OF 75

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a global mechanism for financing climate change mitigation and adaptation projects in developing countries. It aims to support low-carbon, climate-resilient development by providing financial resources to address the impacts of climate change.

### Background
- **Climate Change Impacts**: Rising temperatures, sea level rise, changes in precipitation patterns, and increased frequency of extreme weather events are affecting ecosystems, infrastructure, coastal resources, agriculture, and water systems.
- **Fossil Fuel Use**: Currently, approximately 60% of households in FLM use fossil fuels for cooking. This reliance on fossil fuels contributes to air pollution and greenhouse gas emissions.

### Current Challenges
- **Agriculture and Livelihoods**: The majority of FLM's population is rural, with a high proportion living in coastal regions. Access to basic services such as education, healthcare, and clean water remains limited.
- **Water Security**: Water security is increasingly constrained due to climate change, leading to conflicts over access to water resources.

### Future Projections
- **Population Growth**: By 2050, FLM's population will increase by approximately 1.5 million people, with the majority living in rural areas and coastal regions.
- **Economic Impacts**: The projected economic impact of climate change on FLM is estimated to be $3 billion per year.

### Proposed Solutions
- **Enhanced Adaptation Capacity**: The GCF will work with local authorities and communities to enhance their capacity for adapting to the effects of climate change. This includes strengthening the adaptive capacity of local authorities and communities to ensure resilience against future climate-related shocks.
- **Capacity Building**: The GCF will provide training, technical assistance, and resources to build the capacity of local authorities and communities in areas such as disaster risk management, water resource management, and sustainable land use.

### Implementation
- **Economic Impact**: The proposed project is estimated to have a net present value (NPV) of $10.637 billion.
- **Funding Sources**: The GCF will receive funding from the United States ($9.852 billion), Japan ($4.637 billion), and other sources.

### Project Structure
- **Component 1**: Local authorities empowered to deliver climate change adaptation services in their communities.
- **Component 2**: Priority project implementation-EAD Facility for strengthening local community resilience.

### Conclusion
The GCF is committed to supporting FLM's efforts to adapt to the impacts of climate change and build resilient communities. By providing financial resources and technical assistance, the GCF aims to ensure that FLM can effectively address the challenges posed by climate change.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate Information (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Adaptive context of the Programme area

The Federated States of Micronesia ("FSM") is a widely dispersed archipelago located in the North Pacific Ocean. FSM is formed by four states (Chuuk, Yap, Pohnpei, and Kosrae) and comprises of 607 islands, of which 24 are inhabited. The total land area of FSM is approximately 135 square kilometers with a coastline length of about 2,700 km. Under the 1979 Constitution, FSM has three levels of government: national, state, and municipal. The four states have their own constitutions and governments which are constitutionally autonomous. Each State has its own governor, judiciary and legislative bodies, along with relevant governmental agencies.

As of 2019, FSM's estimated population is 115,021, with a GDP of USD 438 million and a GDP per capita of USD 3,876. The majority (approximately 70%) of the population lives in urban areas while only about one-third live in rural areas. According to the HIES, one requires an average of USD 1.84 per adult per day for basic needs such as food and shelter. This is equivalent to approximately 25% of the national GDP. Approximately half (50%) of the population earns less than USD 3.76 per day while approximately 10% of the population falls below the poverty line and approximately 41% of the population falls below the subsistence level.

The ongoing COVID-19 pandemic is projected to further impact the FSM economy in coming years. Construction, tourism, and other sectors are expected to be negatively affected by the pandemic. The GDP growth rate for overall GDP is expected to have declined by approximately 5% over fiscal year 2020 and to decline by 25% fiscal year 2021.

### Climate change

The climate of FSM displays considerable year-on-year variability due to the Nino-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) phenomenon. The average annual rainfall is estimated at approximately 3,684 mm with a range from as low as 795 mm in October to as high as 10,217 mm between 1917 and 2019.

The climate of FSM is characterized by the extreme climate vulnerability of FSM and the livelihoods of communities, as a large share of the population heavily relies on natural resources. Rising oceans, changing precipitation patterns, and increased frequency of extreme climate events and extended drought periods are expected to have significant impacts on the food and water security of local populations, while posing a serious disaster risk to assets and people.

### Climate trends

The climate trend in FSM is characterized by high temperatures with an average temperature of 27°C, and little variation throughout the year. Average annual precipitation is 3,100 mm, with the months of January to March being drier than other months.

Polynesia experiences a tropical monsoon climate, which varies from month to month. The mean annual rainfall is 4,856 mm, with January and February being slightly drier than other months.

Kosrae's climate is characterized by high temperatures, heavy rainfall and high humidity. The average annual rainfall in Kosrae is approximately 3,000 mm per year. The average temperature of the sea surface at Kosrae is estimated to be as high as 7,500 mm annually with average temperature 27°C at sea level. Average monthly temperatures vary from the annual average by no more than +1°C and the difference between the average minimum and maximum temperatures is less than +4°C.

7. **Temperature:** With less than 1.5°C between its hottest and coldest months, FSM experiences very little seasonal variation in mean air temperatures. Mean annual temperature (1901-2016) is 27.1°C. Annual maximum and minimum temperatures have increased at a rate of approximately 0.38°C per decade since the mid-1950s, with the largest increases occurring in Pohnpei (eastern FSM) and Yap (western FSM). Extreme temperature such as warm days and warm nights have been increasing in Pohnpei consistent with global warming trends. The greatest trends involve minimum air temperatures within FSM. For the State of Pohnpei, the greatest trends involve minimum air temperatures, whereas for Yap, the greater trends are observed in maximum air temperature.

8. **Precipitation:** There is variability between States in changing precipitation patterns. Mean annual precipitation (1901-2016) has increased at a rate of approximately 3.5 mm per decade since the mid-1950s, with the West Pacific Monsoon affecting rainfall in the western islands by contributing additional rain. Generally, there is an increasing trend in Very Wet Day rainfall in Pohnpei (an eastern State) since records began in 1950. There has also been a decreasing trend in Very Wet Day rainfall in Yap (a western State). Consecutive Dry Days in Yap (a western State) have decreased since 1952.

## 9. Sea Level

Since the mid-1990s and especially in FSM, sea level has experienced a diagnostic trend of increasing sea-level rise, ranging from 8 to 12 mm per year, attributed to land areas in the Western Pacific (see Figure 2 below)16). Since 2000, FSM has been increasingly experiencing a periodic rise of sea level in the low-lying coastal areas of both high and low tides. This is due to climate change, which can affect sea-level changes at timescales from months to years. For instance, ENSO can cause sea-level changes on these timescales.

## The ENSO cycle has a profound effect on the distribution of tropical cyclones in the FSM region. During La Nina events, the average number of tropical storms occurs in the FSM region. The formation region of cyclones is also impacted. During El Nino, typhoon formation extends eastward resulting in an increased risk of a typhoon for Palau and Yap. This has been observed during the 2015-2016 El Nino event when two major typhoons hit Palau. On Pohnpei, the risk of having typhoon wind forces of 65 m/s (33.4m-s) or greater is one in ten for El Nino years, and approximately one year in 50 for non-El Nino years.

## Extreme climate events: It is projected that there will be a decrease in the number of typhoons globally by the end of this century, with an increase in average maximum wind speed of typhoons between 2% and 11%, as well as an increase in rainfall intensity of 20% within 100 km of a typhoon's centre. For F3M, the projection for a decrease in frequency of formation is consistent with the observed trend over recent decades. However, there are no projections for extreme rainfall events, such as Tropical Storm Chataan which resulted in massive landslides and damage across Chuuk State during 2015. The projected increase in average maximum wind speed of typhoons will result in an increased probability of extreme rainfall events are projected to increase. This conclusion is based on analysis of daily rainfall data from a subject dataset (Chapter 3) with some improvements (Chapter 1), so the results are slightly different to those in Australian Bureau of Meteorology and Climate Research Centre (BOM) projections. The frequency of days with maximum temperatures above 35°C will increase in line with increases in average temperature; the frequency of such days is also expected to increase.

## Drought: El Niño years in F3M tend to cause droughts which have resulted in water and food shortages (including starvation), loss of livestock, damage to infrastructure, and increased fire risk. These effects are exacerbated by terrestrial habitats, wildfires, and invasive species. ENSO influences the minimum air temperatures in both Yap and Pohnpei. The frequency of days with maximum temperatures in Pohnpei and maximum air temperatures in Yap (all being warmer in La Niña years and colder in La Niño years). The incidence of drought is expected to decrease over the 21st century (except during ENSO

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL v2 | PAGE 11 OF 75

## Vulnerability of the Program area

### Exposure to climate change impacts

- **Ocean acidification and sea surface warming**: Ocean acidification, as measured by the decrease in aragonite saturation, has risen in the region of Micronesia since measurements began in the late 18th century. Studies indicate that ocean acidification will continue to increase at a rate faster than 3.5 mmol/L per decade, indicating an increase in ocean acidification which would severely negatively affect coral reefs and other marine life.
- **Rising sea surface temperatures**: Sea surface temperature is projected to rise by up to 1°C over the next century with a possible increase in the coming decades. Rising sea surface temperatures as well as a projected increase in ocean acidification will have negative impacts on coral reef ecosystems, which are already under stress from disease and pollution.
- **Reduction in migratory fish stocks**: A decrease in the health of these habitats could result in less catch for households that utilize fisheries and marine resources and may lead to increased food insecurity.

### Exposure to extreme weather events

- **Exposure to climate change impacts**: Areas are susceptible to acute climate change risks such as extreme rainfall events, drought, high sea levels, strong winds and extreme high air temperatures. FSM is particularly vulnerable to climate change due to its small size, low elevation, limited freshwater resources, poor infrastructure, geographic remoteness and the fact that FSM remains heavily reliant on the economic assistance under the Compact of Free Association (COFA). The economic outlook beyond 2023 is uncertain and fragile with external shocks.

### Exposure to coastal flooding

- **Coastal flooding**: Approximately 60% of households in FSM live within 180 meters² of the shoreline, with the State of Kiribati having the greatest proportion of its population living near the coast (90%) and Tuvalu having the second highest (94%). 94% of the FSM population lives within at least 1km of the coastline and 100% live within 5 km. Additionally, approximately 87% of structures in FSM are located near or along the coast (see Figure 3). Almost all outer island sites lie within the 2-meter wave run-up zone and 94% lie within the 5-meter zone of storm surge. This poses a significant disaster risk to the majority of households in the project area.

## B

### Main island are also projected to experience coastal changes due to sea level rise by 2055 along with saltwater inundation.

The main island of Micronesia is projected to experience sea-level inundation of low-lying areas up through 2055. The FGF (Funding for Climate Change) project will be implemented in the Federated States of Micronesia. Available [here](#).

### Air temperature

Increased air temperatures are projected to have an impact on food production and FISM (from different emission scenarios). Development and use of climate-resilient crop species and varieties (resistant to heat stress, drought, waterlogging, saltwater, pests, weeds) will be promoted.

### Precipitation

- **Low-lying atolls are more susceptible to sea level rise, storm surge events and storm surges. With enough surface runoff, areas of low elevation can become contaminated and unsuitable for agriculture.**
  - **Demographic shift: migration from low-elevation areas.**
  - **Biodiversity: watershed health decline.**

- **Increased occurrence of intertidal sentinel species (e.g., mangroves) due to the impacts of storm surges, resulting in flooding.**
  - **Flooding: surface runoff, loss of available land for agriculture.**
  - **On the higher islands, increased extreme weather events and sea level rise can result in damage to extensions from existing FISM projects and other infrastructure communications that depend on these areas.**

### Watershed rehabilitation for terrestrial protection and flooding control

- **Watersheds constructed that will reduce the risk of surface runoff and erosion (e.g., use of endemic species planting, water harvesting structures).**
  - **Rehabilitation, restoration or substitution of mangroves in coastal areas (e.g. mangrove restoration, re-vegetation, and protection).**

### Farming and land-use techniques

- **Adoption of agroforestry practices (e.g., mulching, organic farming, mixed cropping systems).**
  - **Establishment of agroforestry plots for food production.**
  - **Building value chains for crops, fisheries and livestock**

## Specific impacts of temperature increase on crops are difficult to predict based on current climate models, but a rise in temperatures by 2°C and beyond will cause significant negative impacts for most crops.

### Negative impacts are projected for rice, wheat, maize, sorghum, millet, cassava (sweet potato), and impact, and even potatoes and turnips (potentially positive).

### Projections for changing climate in the Pacific region vary widely depending on specificities such as rainfall, flooding, scale of agriculture, etc.

## Ocean acidification (pamgite) and ocean heat (sea surface warming)

### Rising sea surface temperatures as well as increased CO2 levels will cause coral bleaching over the coming decades due to climate change. Coral reefs are already under threat from other stressors such as pollution, disease, and overfishing.

### Negative impacts on fisheries and aquaculture are expected especially in near-shore coral reefs where most of the fish species that are important for food security depend on these coral reefs for habitat.

## Natural climatic drivers

### Drought

#### 1. Niuulu La Niña drought

### Increasing evidence of drought is expected; decreases over the 21st century or FEMO conditions are expected to result in drought episodes in the following year, with severe impacts on food security and water availability.

#### Effects:
- **Fisheries:** Significant drop in fish for crop such as coconut, taro and breadfruit
- **Biodiversity:** localized loss of species and occurrence of wildfires
- **Water supply:** Increased demand for water to meet human needs and to import food supplies and bottled water

### The frequency and intensity of droughts are likely to be subject to perceptible reductions due to climate-driven drought. Results of an analysis indicate that out of 105 and islands that have experienced drought in the past, 82% of them experienced a community lasting with a drought condition similar to the drought experienced in 1998.

### Related projects and interventions
27. The following are some of the current and on-going programs and initiatives in FSM that have sought to overcome some of the key climate change challenges previously mentioned. Specifically, the 'Practical Solutions for Reducing Community Vulnerability to Climate Change in FSM' project (hereafter referred to as the Adaptation Fund) is a Pacific-Arctic Climate Fund (PACAFM) projects administered through USAID; the recently approved 'Climate Resilient Food Security for Farming Households across FSM' project and the Small Grants Programme (SGP) project are both funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF); there is ample to increase adaptation capacity and implement sub-grants in FSM through NGOs.

| Project | Program title |
| --- | --- |
|  | **Practical Solutions for Reducing Community Vulnerability to Climate Change in the Federated States of Micronesia** |

### Description
This is a nationwide Adaptation Fund that has an overall goal of increasing the capacity of communities and households across FSM to reduce vulnerability from climate change. The project will work with communities to identify their own priorities for adaptation as well as the increased resilience of communities that have participated.

### Additionality/compatibility with GEF
The present program proposes will work to complement existing projects in FSM, including those funded by USAID and GEF. It is hoped that this project will be able to build on the successes of these other programs and provide a more comprehensive approach to climate change adaptation.

## Climate Resilient Food Security for Farming Communities in the Caribbean and Central America (Trust Fund/GEF Green Climate Fund [GCF])

This additional GCF project is focused on providing a system-wide solution to increasing the resilience of farming communities in the Caribbean and Central America. The vulnerability assessments conducted by the MCT Project will be used as inputs into the design and guide the selection of sub-projects for implementation under this GCF project.

Additionally, as the MCT project does not target smallholder farmers directly, this GCF project will provide financial and technical support to communities in developing countries for small projects that are designed to increase food security while being land-based livelihoods. The programme will be implemented through a combination of FISB and the region, including the implementation of pilot projects.

There is currently limited availability of data and information on the impacts of climate change in FSM because of the small size of the islands, their dispersed nature, and the lack of infrastructure. States have also been tasked with providing information on climate change impacts and adaptation measures to the UNFCCC highlighted? the limited information related to climate base data, vulnerability and adaptation assistance are not being collected in a systematic manner. This is a significant barrier to adaptation. Further, there remains an insufficient scientific and technical knowledge in-country to effectively address climate change issues.

## Technical and Institutional Challenges

### Limited local awareness of climate change from households and community members which leads to limited buy-in for adaptation priorities: Outcomes of the stakeholder engagement process revealed limited awareness of the linkages between climate change and impacts on GHG and carbon protection, food security and water security.

#### Private sector capacity and involvement
- **Limited experience in designing, implementing and managing climate change adaptation projects and building resilience to climate change:** A study conducted by the World Bank in 2013 found that although municipality capacity varies within and between States, in general, municipalities were weak in terms of human resources, equipment, tools, finance and procurement systems.
- **Limited experience in designing, implementing and managing climate change adaptation projects and building resilience to climate change:** A study conducted by the World Bank in 2013 found that although municipality capacity varies within and between States, in general, municipalities were weak in terms of human resources, equipment, tools, finance and procurement systems.
- **Private sector capacity and involvement:** The Chamber of Commerce has a role in mobilizing the private sector in its consultations for the preparation of the Country Program. The Chamber of Commerce is a member of the FSB Climate Change Working Group. The Chamber of Commerce has been involved with the GCF in the successful engagement of the private sector in climate change investments require transformative economies of scale, and that the private sector can play an important role by engaging its private sector with regards to mobilization of capital from the private sector or active collaboration towards the development of innovative financing mechanisms.
- **Private sector capacity and involvement:** The Chamber of Commerce has a role in mobilizing the private sector in its consultations for the preparation of the Country Program. The Chamber of Commerce is a member of the FSB Climate Change Working Group. The Chamber of Commerce has been involved with the GCF in the successful engagement of the private sector in climate change investments require transformative economies of scale, and that the private sector can play an important role by engaging its private sector with regards to mobilization of capital from the private sector or active collaboration towards the development of innovative financing mechanisms.

#### Local climate change adaptation capacity is not sustained effectively: Past attempts at projects have largely been focused on building capacity in a narrow range of areas, such as disaster risk reduction and supporting vulnerable communities in the face of climate change. While some level of capacity is built during the implementation phase of these projects, this capacity is often lost when the project ends because there are no mechanisms or challenges include the brain drain, general level of educational attainment in the various states, the standard of education, access to domestic educational opportunities or resources.

#### Financial barrier
- **Insufficient available financing from alternative sources to develop and implement locally relevant adaptation strategies:** While there is a growing recognition that climate change adaptation needs development, but the funding gap remains too large to meet adaptation needs considering the magnitude of potential climate change impacts. Further more, there are significant gaps in the availability of financial resources for adaptation actions which creates a void for effective and coordinated financing for climate adaptation in FSB communities.

#### Institutional barrier
- **Uncoordinated government adaptation planning and integration into national policy frameworks:** While development plans at the State level have been developed to address climate change impacts, they are not early enough. In FSB States, low adaptive capacity is further constrained by weak linkages among different levels of governance (national, sub-national and local) and will be undermined.

#### Social barrier
- **Traditional social structures and concepts can create complexities for the implementation of certain adaptation strategies:** For example, in some communities, women are not allowed to own property. emphasis matrilineal descent where titles, rites and acquisition to property are traced through female hereditary lines. This creates a situation where women have limited access to land and resources. In addition, there is traditional matrilineal descent to a system with most decision-making related to land ownership and land use being done by men.
- **Social barrier:** Polynesian - Private landowners have final authority on private land. However, any conflict goes through the Department of Land and Natural Resources. The State has the final authority for all state lands territorial waters and marine resources. States make final decisions on the use and conservation of marine resources or the designated protected areas.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 19 OF 75

## B

**Ko'olau vs. Koeaer:** Koearae has similar laws to Pohnpei regarding land ownership. Koearae Land Court has procedures to resolve conflict interests regarding the use of private lands. For public lands, the use of these resources is subject to consultation with the state and the municipality where the resource is located.

- **Yap -** Yap, land ownership at the municipal level (municipality) involving the owners, titled estates of the place, and traditional leader of the place. On land/ownership/resource conflict, if there are two or more parties involved in a dispute over land ownership, the court will hear from the chief of each municipality with appointed judges from the place). The State Court would defer it back to the local government for resolution.

- **Chuuk -** Land ownership and near shore marine resources are privately owned. However, Chuuk has laws that allow the state to intervene in land/ownership/resource conflicts. If there is a conflict goes through the court system but authority on conservation and natural resource use on privately owned lands remains with the owner. The State Court will hear from representatives of communities and owners near shore marine resources for conservation and natural resource use.

Micro-level barriers to adaptation include lack of knowledge and understanding of climate change and adaptation programming.

## Theory of change

The proposed EDA-Programme’s Theory of Change (ToC) builds on the principle that reducing the vulnerability of local communities to climate change impacts is a key component for sustainable development. This ToC outlines how climate change impacts on their livelihoods, and subsequently to design and implement climate adaptation solutions relating to these impacts. The ToC also describes how the Programme will address the current barriers to achieving transformational change.

Component 1 supports a variety of capacity assessment and capacity building activities to ensure that LAs are able to identify and prioritize local adaptation priorities at the community level, and then develop and implement effective projects related to their community local adaptation priorities. The key output results delivered by this component will be the identification of local adaptation priorities and the development and implementation of projects. Component 2 leverages this foundation to support the implementation of priority adaptation projects by the Life Adaptation Fund (LAF) in the three pilot communities. These projects are expected to have a positive impact on these communities in three critical sectors based on the prioritization process (more details on the prioritization process can be found in Annex 4). The projects will address climate change impacts and vulnerabilities, but are likely to include uptake of diversified, climate resilient livelihood options, improved food security and water security, increased resilience against disasters, reduced economic and human costs from disaster/climate change, and overall increased resilience of target communities.

## During the programme design phase, a survey was conducted with LAs to assess their absorption capacity in light of the current and programmed funding received. Generally, municipalities have budgets ranging from USD 10,000 up to USD 500,000. The average annual grant amount is USD 10k. For more information on the survey conducted, please see Annex 4 of the Feasibility Study.

#### Component 1: Local authorities empowered to deliver climate change adaptation services to their populations

##### 32 Across FSM, LAs (municipalities and the State-level governments) are well-suited to coordinate and develop local level adaptation strategies and plans. The project will support LAs in developing a comprehensive suite of technical assistance resources reach each beneficiary communities. The beneficiary assessment (See Annex 2, section 5) identifies that the most pressing needs for climate change adaptation services at the community level can be categorized by the different forms of human, financial, and environmental capital.

##### Component 1 delivers three outputs to strengthen the capacities of LAs across FSM:

- **Output 1.1** will directly address barriers such as the limited scientific and technical knowledge and understanding of climate change vulnerability and adaptation options for climate change adaptation programming.
- **Output 1.2** will support LAs in preparing climate change adaptation projects targeting the project’s Resilient Communities Grant Facility (RCGF) to improve access to climate financing.
- **Funding:** USD 500,000
- **Component 1 activities will be implemented by a dedicated ECU that will be established under component and hosted within SPC regional office in Fiji. The ECU will have the dual responsibility to run the day-to-day operations of the ECU and manage the RCGF under component 2 under the responsibility of SPC as the Executing Entity.

##### Output 1.1 Build technical expertise to identify and prioritize adaptation solutions

- **Activity A:** Develop a comprehensive training curriculum for LAs empowering them with the scientific and technical knowledge and understanding of climate change vulnerability and adaptation options to identify and prioritize adaptation actions.
- **Activity B:** Develop an integrated decision support framework and training curriculum including interactive case studies targeting government officials beginning in 2019.
- **Activity C:** Ensure a transformation in the way LAs’s and state agencies identify and prioritize domestic adaptation measures. By 2023, develop an adaptation measure prioritization framework, climate risk screening tools and

Existing tools such as the UNDP’s Gender Action Plan Tool™; the UNDP Toolkit for Practitioners on Gender and Climate Adaptation Initiatives; the CRISTA tool™, and the USAID’s Pacific Islands Development Program Small Grants Guide® will be incorporated into the following project components:

### Activity 1.1.1: Gender Response design incorporated into sub-grant development training; localized assessment of gender-responsive adaptation needs

Activity 1.1.2 Climate adaptation decision support training for local authorities (2 trainings per State)

Implementing parties[ECU, ECU, GCF]

The project will leverage knowledge and resources identified and provide an introduction to the EDA Facility to familiarize local government officials with the objectives of the facility, the process, eligibility criteria, and details about how to access funding. The training will be conducted in two phases:

1. **Phase 1:** Training for 20 representatives (approximately 140 people). At least two (2) representatives from relevant State agencies will also be trained (departmental level). These representatives will then train other local government officials and community members (approximately 140 people).
2. **Phase 2:** Training of trainers. This will incorporate the following gender-responsive sub-action from the Gender Action Plan:
   - Activity 3.1.1: Develop a training manual on gender-mainstreaming for climate resilience, for local authorities/staff and elected and appointed officials.

Activity 1.1.3 Develop train-the-trainer’s module and conduct training for national consultants and/or local NGOs in technical areas

Implementing parties[ECU]

Two facilitators from each of the four states will be selected and procured by SPC through a competitive process to serve as technical support facilitating Agents (FA). They will provide capacity and project implementation assistance, including but not limited to: providing guidance on how to access funding; identifying sources of expertise, jobs or title track record delivering on projects or initiatives in food security, water security, DRR and coastal resilience; and ensuring that the EDA Facility is aware of any potential climate change adaptation projects. To ensure FA can support LAs in the prioritization and development of climate change projects, particularly for the EDA Facility. FAs will be added to a roster of experts and paired with LAs by SPC based on FA expertise and experience.

Activity 1.1.4 Facilitating Agents to support LAs prioritize adaptation actions for Grant Facility

In addition to building climate resilience, the EDA Facility, through the awarded grants, will assist in strengthening the capacity of LAs to identify, prioritize and implement projects that are aligned with their climate change adaptation needs. The recipient LAs develop sufficient capacity to implement adaptation sub-grants and sustain the country's climate-resilient development efforts.

Modeling, feasibility analysis and design of sub-grants will ultimately be driven by the contact with LAs and State governments to ensure that the project is aligned with their needs and that it does not weaken the EDA approach of building the capacity of LAs to identify, prioritize and implement projects.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 28 OF 75

## THEMATIC AREAS AND ADAPTATION INTERVENTIONS FOR THE EDA PROGRAMME

### Thematic area description
**Climate change impacts:** Increased coastal erosion, sea level rise, and increased frequency of extreme weather events (e.g., hurricanes, tropical storms) are projected to increase the vulnerability of coastal communities. DRR sub-projets: Community-led, that can safeguard lives, livelihoods, and assets in a sustainable manner. For example, projects for the area such as projects could expect for extreme weather events, sea level rise, and increased frequency of extreme weather events (e.g., hurricanes, tropical storms). In some cases they play a role in reducing extreme events, and as such be considered adaptation interventions. However, in other cases, these interventions will need to be linked to broader climate change mitigation efforts or may not provide a direct link to a level of healthy and functioning ecosystems.

### Adaptation Interventions
**Resulting existing buildings:** Climate-proofing against increased sea levels and extreme weather events (e.g., hurricanes, tropical storms). Water harvesting for land-based protection and flood risk reduction. Small-scale coastal infrastructure built that will reduce the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts (e.g., in areas of use, adoption of new species, planting wave breakers, etc.). Coastal wetlands restoration and mangrove reforestation for adaptation (e.g., mangrove restoration, re-vegetation, sea walls). Equipping municipalities with necessary tools to build resilience against extreme weather events. Climate-proofing existing buildings, roads, bridges, public health, medical and other supplies.

**Food security sub-projets:** Address the management of cropland, livestock, fisheries, aquaculture, and agroforestry systems in a manner that is climate-resilient (e.g., through the use of drought-resistant crops and varieties). Development and use of climate-resilient crop species and varieties. Climate-proofing existing agricultural infrastructure including technologies for their consistent supply (irrigation systems, etc.). Farm and land-use techniques facilitating soil and water conservation. Establishment of agroforestry systems that are climate resilient. Small-scale aquaculture systems. Livestock management. Improved waste management. Establishment of agroforestry sites integrated with livestock production.

**Water security sub-projets:** Interventions that address increased demand for water, including the development and expansion of water supplies, especially in the coastal areas; increased incidence of natural disasters such as droughts, floods, and storms (e.g., in the case of Chukot, Pomeria, and Krasnoyarsk). Water security measures to ensure access to safe drinking water. Production and distribution of containerized collection systems. Establishment of rainwater harvesting and water storage facilities. Water supply management (e.g., introducing compact toilets, demand management through awareness raising).

**Output 2 Establishment of Resilient Community Grant Facility (RCGF)**
The RCGF will provide EDA funding for the implementation of climate change adaptation measures. The output will put in place the relevant requirements, rules, and regulations for the RCGF. It will be established as a separate fund within the GCF project. The RCGF will be managed by the GCF Secretariat in accordance with the Operations Manual, operating under the legal name of SPC as the single executing entity for this project.

The indicative list of interventions for each thematic area will be refined by the governing bodies at the outset of the project.

If the project is evaluated as either “high-capacity” or “low-capacity”, the project will be evaluated as “low capacity” will be able to choose to receive additional support either from a State agency or from SPAs, as appropriate.

* Option 1: The lower capacity LA (in this case, usually a municipality) would be matched with a higher capacity LA. In such cases, the ECU will provide technical training and support for the EAS in the role of the higher capacity LA at the lead institution.
* Option 2: The lower capacity LA may engage an external professional and/or provider and directly support project implementation in collaboration with the LA (in this case, usually a municipality).

## B

### Implementation of the project

At the conclusion of implementation, all grant awards will be evaluated, and lessons gathered and captured in a common format with tailored and gender-sensitive adaptation measures, completed lessons learned reports and other relevant documentation will be made available on the GCF website and/or through the GCF’s country portal/website. As detailed in Section 6 – Financial and progress reporting of the Operations Manual, the evaluation of sub-proposals may be carried out either by the ECU or the GCF, with support from the Facilitating Agency.

### Lessons learned

The lessons learned will be gathered through a combination of workshops and meetings by LAs and FIs. This is structured and systematized by the ECU. Information will be disseminated throughout a range of climate change and other information channels including but not limited to: GCF website, GCF country portal, GCF’s regional hubs such as GEOPONIC, the Pacific Data Hub**, etc.,**. This will be undertaken by the ECU in partnership with and coordination with DCS/GEOPONIC.

### Executing Entity and Other Implementing Partners

#### 77. SPC as the sole Executing Entity for the proposed programme.

A number of units, government departments and other stakeholders will contribute to the operational management of the EDA Programmes as well as the implementation of the project activities. The SPC will be responsible for the overall coordination and implementation of the project through the executing entity. These additional implementing partners have been selected in collaboration with the Government of FSM through discussions at the Project Preparation Phase.

**EDA Programme Coordination Unit:** A dedicated ECU will be created within the SPC regional office in FSM, including a Programme Coordinator, Programme Administrative Assistant, Finance and Procurement Officer, and other staff as required. The ECU will be responsible for the overall coordination of the project, including a communication expert. SPC as the Executing Entity will be legally responsible for the acts and decisions of the EDA Programmes.

**EDA Project Implementation Unit:** A dedicated PIU will be created within the SPC regional office in FSM to implement the EDA Programmes and manage the RGF. The ECU will coordinate and implement the programme activities, budget, and reporting requirements with the PIU. The PIU will be responsible for the day-to-day implementation of the Executing Entity, separately but with differentiated responsibilities from but under the oversight of SPC's GCF programme management.

#### 78. FSM Department of Finance and Administration: Recruitment of one staff using GCF resources to support the EDA Programmes. The recruitment process will be coordinated by the SPC, the NDC, and the SPA. The NDC works alongside SPC’s CCES and ECU to establish the EDA Programme's governing structure, including the establishment of a National Project Board (NPB) for the project. In-and co-financing from the Government of FSM will support the functions carried out by this staff.

#### 79. State GCF Focal Points: Each State GCF Focal Points will support the ECU and NDC in disseminating information, providing technical assistance to the SPC, and supporting the implementation of project activities, trainings and workshops.

#### 80. EDA Programme Board (EPB): The EPB will be a national body chaired by the DCA and responsible for taking corrective action as needed to ensure the EDA Programme achieves the desired results. The Board membership includes representatives from the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Emergency Management and NGO representatives. The FSM National Project Board (NPB) is responsible for ensuring that the EPB meets its responsibilities. The NDC will provide a quality assurance and advisory role and will support the EPU and ECJ by carrying out independent Programme oversight and monitoring functions. The NPB ensures appropriate project management missions are monitored and completed. The Programme assurance role is totally independent of the Programme implementation function. SPC as the Executing Entity will be responsible for the acts and decisions of the EPB.

The EPB will meet once a year with the possibility to hold virtual meetings for final sub-grant approvals every 6 months or as needed. Specific responsibilities of the EPB include:

- Ensure that the EDA Programme is implemented in accordance with its approved terms of reference.
- Ensure that all project activities are carried out within any remaining specific constraints and advice on or address project amendments, issues raised and grievances.

#### 81. Project Implementation Unit (PIU): The PIU will be responsible for implementing the programme and managing actions:

- Ensure coordination between various donor, government-funded projects and government agencies.
- Track and monitor the Programme progress, assess performance, co-finance, ensure that the agreed deliverables are met on time and in accordance with the approved terms of reference.
- Approve the project inception Report. Mid-term Review and Terminal Evaluation reports and corresponding recommendations for approval by the EPB.
- Receipt and oversight of addressing of grievances raised through the grievance redress mechanism.

#### 79. In case consensus cannot be reached within the Board, the SPC Representative (or their designee) will make a decision on behalf of the Board. The NDC will ensure that the decision-making process is not unduly delayed. This is in accordance with the role of SPC as the sole executing entity for the project.

80. SPC-CCES will serve in the quality assurance role and will support the EPU and ECJ by carrying out objective and independent Programme oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management missions are managed and completed. The Programme assurance role is totally independent of the Programme

### Funding Flows for the EDA Programme
(1) The funding flows for the EDA Programme are illustrated in Figure 8. As the Accredited Entity, SPC's CCES Division – through its CFU – will receive funds from the GCF. These funds, along with the in-and co-financing from GoF STM and SPC/CCES, will be disbursed to the executing entity. The executing entity is responsible for managing the RCGF. Based on the applications received and the capacities of the accredited entity, the executing entity may either directly manage the RCGF or delegate this responsibility to an EDA partner. If the executing entity delegates the responsibility to an EDA partner, it will be responsible for monitoring the activities of the EDA partner and ensuring that the funds are used as intended.

(2) The disbursement of funds to successful grantees will be processed according to SPC's Finance Regulations (2018), Finance Policy: Procurement (2020), Finance Policy: Grants and Sub-delegations (2020) and Finance Policy: Anti-Money Laundering and Combating Financing (2020). These policies ensure considerable internal controls and due diligence so that funds provided to grantees are properly disbursed and managed.

**Record track of the AAD and EEU**

91. The Pacific Community has been supporting development and environmental protection in the Pacific region since its establishment in Noumea, New Caledonia, and comprising 26 sovereign states and territories.

92. As an international organization works for the effective and innovative application of science and knowledge to improve living conditions in the Pacific Islands Region, the SPG is committed to ensuring the long-term resilience of local ecosystems and communities. SPG's track record on the elaboration, implementation and management of climate change adaptation projects in the Pacific region is well established.

93. The pipeline and strategic direction of projects is identified in the Pacific Community Strategic Plan 2016-2020.

94. SPG’s Micronesia Regional Office, located in Palikir, Pohnpei, Federated States of Micronesia (FSM), is the main hub for SPG's projects and programs in the region. The regional office has supported the development of climate change adaptation strategies for Palau and territories of the Northern Mariana Islands (CNMI) and Guam. The regional office has supported a number of projects, including the Pacific Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (PCCAS), the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Small Grants Program (SGP) in the Federated States of Micronesia (FSM), the Pacific Islands Micro-Insurance Project (PIMIP), and the Pacific Community’s Pacific Climate Change Alliance Pacific Secretariat (PCCAS).

95. FSM Department of Finance and Administration (GCF NDA) has been the recipient of two rounds of GCF resources, with a total amount of USD 10 million for climate change adaptation projects in the country.

96. The GCF is currently developing its second assessment for FSM, which will be used to develop the third assessment for DAEs. It is expected that the development of GCF proposals, including IPFDR and more recently an SGP project on disaster risk management, and an EEU project on climate change adaptation will help to meet the needs of the least resilient communities.

97. The project will overcome critical barriers to strengthen the resilience of the most vulnerable population to the impacts of extreme climatic events as well as long-term water and food insecurity as a result of predicted climate change in FSM. It will also contribute to improving the capacity of local authorities, civil society organizations, management, food and water security for FSM’s most vulnerable populations.

98. AAs are defined by their location, which is determined by geography, natural environment and socio-political context. Further, AAs also in a position that enables them the responsibility to manage and regulate resources within their jurisdiction. The AA is therefore an important actor in climate change adaptation.

99. These authorities are ideally positioned to effectively coordinate between: on-the-ground stakeholders and key sectors; national governments and international organizations; and between different levels of government, from strengthening resilience and adapting capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in all countries. This role is critical for the success of any climate change adaptation project.

100. The GCF will support AAs in FSM by providing technical assistance, capacity building, financial resources and reinforcing measures for reducing weather- and climate-related risks*^1^.

101. Specifically, the GCF will provide funding to: (i) build the capacity of AAs in FSM; (ii) increase the capacity of AAs in FSM’s DDR capacity and agricultural water sector; GCF funds will be used specifically to address critical information and capacity gaps of AAs as well as cover the additional cost of climate change adaptation. Indeed, the current capacity gap is significant for AAs in FSM, which are located on low-lying islands and vulnerable States. This highlights the necessity of the proposed GCF grant structure and concessionally to directly support local communities.

102. GCF grant support is required to address a number of key market failures in FSM. Firstly, the majority of outputs and outcomes of climate change adaptation projects are not marketed or sold on the open market for which benefits accrue to many beneficiaries and are often un-monetized or it is difficult to distinguish intrinsic


---
**Page 43**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 41 OF 75

B

financial returns on financial returns are unattractive. While some adaptation options may ultimately result in increased revenue generation for individuals, it is often difficult to quantify specific returns on grant investments especially when considering the magnitude of potential climate change impacts. Specifically, a previous capacity assessment for FCM found that only about 10% of the total amount of funding available from the GCF would be leveraged at the State and local level currently far outweighs departmental budgets and that there is limited capacity in national and State governments to effectively manage and implement climate change adaptation projects. The amount of funds leveraged in this space, but the funding amount is still inadequate to meet adaptation needs and furthermore than 60% of the total GCF portfolio is concentrated on mitigation activities. This highlights the need for greater coordinated financing for climate adaptation in FCM communities. All of this highlights the important need for GCF grant projects to be designed with a strong focus on co-financing.

### Concessionality

The table shows the maximum possible level of concessionalality in accordance with FCM's economic, institutional and fiscal constraints and specificities. Although the Government of FSM is taking actions to build a policy and legislative framework for climate change adaptation, it has not yet been able to fully implement these policies which are largely dependent on ODA. are currently insufficient to achieve the meaningful reductions in climate change impacts that are needed. The current level of concessionalality is 50% of FCM's public debt. This charge has thus far been tilted towards mitigation. FSM’s public debt, all of which is external, is currently low at around $12 million. However, this is expected to increase significantly in the next few years due to an upward trajectory from 2024. FSM’s debt is assessed as high-risk-of-debt distress. Compounding the high risk of debt distress potential climate change impacts could have a significant negative impact on growth and debt sustainability. Damages to public infrastructure and private property, including homes, businesses, and other assets, can lead to increased costs for government services post-disaster rehabilitation and reconstruction of damaged infrastructure would lead to higher fiscal deficit and public debt levels. The average cost of natural disasters in FSM is estimated at $10 million per year with an average recurrence interval of 100 years. The Pacific Catastrophe Risk Financing Initiative (PCRFI) estimates that it is likely that FCM will incur losses from natural disaster events exceeding $50,000 annually and up to $200,000 in any given year. FSM has a 50% chance of experiencing natural disaster losses exceeding USD 105 million and casualties larger than 300 people per year. ### Debt Sustainability

Given the debt trajectory from 2024, financial instruments such as loans and guarantees are not appropriate for financing climate change adaptation projects. The GCF’s International Monetary Fund (IMF) Climate Change Policy Assessment indicates that further mobilizing external grant financing is therefore crucial to implement the climate change adaptation strategy. This assessment also highlights the importance of a strong focus on implementation of adaptation investment projects. # B Capacity building and developing a track record of project execution and financial management

102. GCF will support capacity development for LAs to implement projects and capacity enhancement efforts from the project inception. Each sub-grant and main grant include an exit strategy, demonstrating the long-term sustainability of the proposed project intervention and how the project will provide long-term resilience in the face of climate change. The exit strategy should be developed during the project preparation phase and reviewed during implementation and well before project closure through activity 2.34 which will not only include the development of an exit strategy but also a review of the lessons learned from the project.

103. The EDA model from this Programme has the strong potential to catalyse additional financing for FSMs and its LAs, including through the mobilization of domestic resources. This is achieved by developing a credible track record of project execution, and crucial capacity/hypocrisy enhancing effective project and financial management systems. The EDA model also provides an opportunity to unlock future projects and investment for FSM and the LA.

104. The EDA model will be based on the generation of environmental returns but not cash returns. These investments are likely to attract significant flows of private finance during the implementation of the proposed EDA model. To ensure that this happens, it is important that all stakeholders involved in the project actions are invited to engage in consultations and workshops to learn about the opportunities and successes generated by the programme and the implementation of sub-grants. Indeed, resilient livelihoods, infrastructure and ecosystem services will be strengthened through the implementation of the EDA model. This will lead to increased trading activity, agriculture, processing, fisheries etc. The improvement of ecosystem services will directly contribute to poverty reduction and improved food security in the long term.

105. Given the primary source of revenue for the government of FSM is financial revenues under the terms of the Compact Agreement with the United States. This Compact was re-instituted in 2020. Given the level of uncertainty and the highly political nature of the decision, it is impossible for the government of FSM to commit to any specific amount of funds that will be allocated to the EDA model. Therefore there is limited potential for allocation of tax or other revenues to the RCGF at this stage, particularly with the envisaged increase in public spending on climate change adaptation and mitigation as part of the decision making. State and municipal governments may in a position to later agree on allocating local revenues to the RCGF.

106. Further and as acknowledged in the GCF Country Programmes, the extent of private sector engagement in the context of FSM is currently limited to projects or programmes which have a private sector development component; rather than being an integral part of the project design. The private sector is not yet fully integrated into the CP and in the Funding Proposal and its annexes, the private sector in FSM is relatively small and underdeveloped. However, there are opportunities for increased private sector engagement as the EDA model and other sector actors face important constraints such as high operational and transport costs, which limits their ability to engage with the public sector.

107. Given these constraints, the project will seek opportunities to build awareness within the private sector of opportunities in FSM and how they can be engaged more fully in climate change adaptation projects through consultations and workshops – as is already happening under climate change projects in FSM - to learn about the

opportunities and successes generated by the programme and the implementation of sub-grants (e.g. Activities 1.1.2, 1.3.2 and 1.3.3). Bidders will be encouraged to involve local communities, as the EOI assessment and sub-grants assessments criteria consider inclusion of the involvement of local private sector entities in bids. As the programme matures, it is expected that the GCF will increasingly support the development of a market for resilience building activities and the ownership of processes by national and local governments, communities and the private sector to secure sustainable financing for such activities.

108. After the completion of the GCF funding period for the EDA programme, the RCFG will be handed over from the GCF to the Government of FSM. Moreover, the relevant guidance on the implementation of sub-grants under the RCFG will be provided by the GCF in line with the EDA programme.

Upon completion of the EDA programme, the EGU will be absorbed into a relevant FSM government institution – provisionally identified as the Department of Finance and Administration and within the remit of the NDA office. All existing agreements and arrangements between the GCF and the EGU will remain in place with these actions being taken by order of the Government of FSM. Any future role that SPC may have in ongoing sub-grants under the RCFG will be considered based on a request from the Government of FSM to that effect.

**Note:** The project will support the implementation of DRR, climate change adaptation and water security adaptation solutions for some of the most pressing needs of vulnerable communities. Component 1 will support three main objectives: (i) building capacity to implement climate change adaptation activities; (ii) supporting the activities that fund capacity-building are conducted in stages to support local climate governance. The first will build technical capacity at national level, including through training and workshops, as well as providing technical assistance for prioritizing adaptation solutions (Activity 1.1.1 and 1.1.2) reinforced through practical hands-on support to promote the development of a national adaptation plan (NAP). The second will provide technical assistance to NGOs in each state to become FAS to support sub-national entities (As) to prepare pre-grant adaptation projects. While a number of capacity-building activities are envisaged, these will be implemented in stages and across different areas of focus. The project will also build the capacity of national authorities across FSM and will be linked with regional knowledge platforms. In the second stage, organizational audits for each state and LAs will be conducted to determine whether the applicant has the necessary capacity to implement the grant. This will include a review of the capacities and a likelihood assessment that is implemented through training to strengthen areas such as financial management, risk management, control (2.2.1), and governance processes as well as project management, M&E, reporting, finance and procurement (2.3.1).


---
**Page 48**
---

113\. The project will both develop, as well as transfer a set of nationally appropriate DRR, food and water security adaptation technologies and practices that would ensure the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable populations. Detailed descriptions of the technology and practice prototypes are presented in Appendix F of Annex 2 and will provide gender-sensitive impact including:

- Support to communities to address water insecurity and promote climate-resilient agriculture (not widely practiced in FSM).
- Rehabilitation of degraded land for food production (practiced at a very low level in some areas of FSM).
- ITTA-ALLEY farming system to address food insecurity (where tree-legume gardens and other agro-forestry are practiced in FSM, the ITTA-ALLEY technique has not been introduced).
- Sustainable water harvesting techniques for improved water security (being practiced in some areas of FSM).
- Drip or micro-irrigation to address water insecurity and promote climate-resilient agriculture (not widely practiced in FSM).
- Offshore structures for DRR (being practiced in some areas of FSM).
- Mangrove rehabilitation and protection for DRR (being practiced in some areas of FSM).

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 114.
The proposed EDA Programme contributes to enabling climate-resilient sustainable development by improving the resilience of vulnerable people and communities, infrastructure and the built environment as well as ecosystems at LA II levels. It does so by empowering municipalities and States in FSM to foster climate change adaptation at their respective scales, and by addressing top-priority vulnerabilities through the implementation of focal projects in target sectors.

##### 115.
As the exact nature and scale of the sub-grants is to be supported by the EDA Grant Facility underpinning the proposed EDA Programme, it is not possible to provide a precise estimate of the number of direct beneficiaries below. As a whole, it is anticipated that the Programme will indirectly benefit 80% of FSMs through the increased capacity and resources provided for climate change adaptation.

##### 116.
For food security, the share of the total population depending on agricultural occupations for their livelihoods was derived using the shared agricultural workers from the 2015 CSA. Adaptation measures would aim to address top-priority vulnerabilities in the following areas: water availability and access; soil fertility; crop yields; the number of potential beneficiaries for food security adaptation interventions is 19,819 people (of which 10,077 males and 9,742 females) living within 180 meters of the shoreline.

##### 117.
For water security, beneficiaries' estimates were derived using the share of surveyed households who had access to improved water sources. Adaptation measures would aim to address top-priority vulnerabilities in the following areas: water availability and access; sanitation and hygiene; health and nutrition; the number of potential direct beneficiaries for water security adaptation interventions is 28,678 people (of which 10,514 males and 10,165 females) living within 180 meters of the shoreline.

##### 118.
For DRR and coastal protection adaptation measures, aiming at addressing impacts from sea-level rise and extreme weather events, beneficiaries' estimates were derived using the share of surveyed households who had access to improved water sources. Adaptation measures would aim to address top-priority vulnerabilities in the following areas: water availability and access; sanitation and hygiene; health and nutrition; the number of potential direct beneficiaries for DRR interventions is 13,803 people (of which 7,218 males and 6,585 females) living within 180 meters of the shoreline. In line with the share of beneficiaries compared to the total FSM population, it is anticipated that the proposed EDA Programme will indirectly benefit 80% of FSMs through increased capacity and resources provided for climate change adaptation.

##### 119.
The sum of the potential number of direct beneficiaries is 54,351, accounting for 82.7% of the total FSM population. The indirect beneficiaries, proposed EDA Programme will indirectly benefit 60% of the total FSM population.

##### 120.
All 75 municipalities, including those with councils of chiefs primarily in smaller municipalities, will be targeted by the proposed EDA Programme. The EDA Facility has been structured and designed to enable all municipalities, even those with small populations, to access funding for climate change adaptation projects. The EDA Facility will provide financial support from State agencies or SPDC directly to implement adaptation solutions targeting the most vulnerable groups in FSMs. It is anticipated that the proposed EDA Programme will indirectly benefit 80% of FSMs through increased capacity and resources provided for climate change adaptation.

##### 121.
The beneficiaries' selection criteria are based on GCF's criteria. This will be assessed as part of the Sub-Grant Review process, which covers 13–20 and 24–29 and deal with various aspects of beneficiary selection (see Appendix VII to Annex 21 Operations Manual).

#### D

**121.** Ultimately, the Programme will work to shift the status quo from a pathway of climate vulnerability, elevated health risks and food insecurity for vulnerable communities in FSM to one of improved food security, enhanced DDR and recovery, and improved socioeconomic development by building local capacity and resilience through the implementation of adaptation actions that are consistent with the goals set out in the FSM National Climate Change Strategy and contribute to four of the GCF’s adaptation results areas.

All of these actions will be implemented at scale within the most vulnerable people, communities and regions. Through the provision of technical assistance and capacity building, the Programme will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy with adaptive capacity, and to prepare viable project proposals which will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term. All of the EDA Programme’s thematic areas and proposed adaption interventions aim to alleviate the impacts of climate change on the most vulnerable people, communities and regions.

**GCF adaptation results area 2 (A2) – Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security:** The delivery of sub-grants from the EDA Facility Fund will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term. Through the provision of technical assistance and capacity building, the Programme will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy with adaptive capacity, and to prepare viable project proposals which will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term.

**GCF adaptation results area 4 (A4) – Increased resilience of infrastructure and the built environment:** The delivery of sub-grants from the EDA Facility Fund will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term. Through the provision of technical assistance and capacity building, the Programme will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy with adaptive capacity, and to prepare viable project proposals which will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term.

**GCF adaptation results area 6 (A6) – Increased resilience of infrastructure and the built environment:** The delivery of sub-grants from the EDA Facility Fund will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term. Through the provision of technical assistance and capacity building, the Programme will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy with adaptive capacity, and to prepare viable project proposals which will directly contribute to increasing the resilience of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions in both the short-term and long term.

**122.** Although the risk of maladaptation exists in all adaptation solutions, adaptation actions that seek to increase sensitivity (as defined by Component 3 of the EDA Programme) can carry a medium risk. In this light, the sub-grant screening form include several provisions designed to mitigate the risk of maladaptation and ensure that the proposed adaptation action is consistent with the GCF’s climate change adaptation strategy.

Special Risk activities have been included (Appendix1 of Appendix 2 in F3) to exclude potential sub-grants with associated risks, including those that are not aligned with the GCF’s climate change adaptation strategy as well as the displacement of people.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

**123.** The entire focus of the EDA Programme and its sub-grants is centered on empowering local communities and States to develop and tailor climate change projects based on their specific needs and priorities. The implementation of the proposed Programme will lead to a paradigm shift away from systemic limits in capacity and resources that have historically constrained the ability of FSM’s most vulnerable people, communities and regions to adapt and technical ability to translate communities’ identified needs into viable climate adaptation projects. By addressing these constraints, the EDA Programme will enable the execution of innovative climate adaptation projects that adequately address impacts identified at the local level; therefore guaranteeing the sustainability of the GCF investment and its potential to generate long-lasting

### Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

The sub-grants will focus on leveraging best available technology and approaches for the disaster risk reduction (DRR) adaptation projects in the Pacific region. The Programme will also seek to catalyse local and national policy change by demonstrating the effectiveness of small-scale, locally designed adaptation projects, and provides incentives for a greater budget allocation to climate change adaptation activities at the national level. By focusing on communities that are most vulnerable to climate impacts to develop and implement their own priority adaptation projects, these efforts will drive community ownership over the long-term. The Programme will also seek to catalyse local and national policy change by demonstrating the effectiveness of small-scale, locally designed adaptation projects, and provides incentives for a greater budget allocation to climate change adaptation activities at the national level.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The Programme will see to catalyse local and national policy change by demonstrating the effectiveness of small-scale, locally designed adaptation projects, and provides incentives for a greater budget allocation to climate change adaptation activities at the national level. By focusing on communities that are most vulnerable to climate impacts to develop and implement their own priority adaptation projects, these efforts will drive community ownership over the long-term (e.g., green/blue coastal infrastructure, rainwater systems, etc.) and behaviour change in response to climate change. In addition to catalysing local policy change through demonstration projects, the EDA Programme will also help to develop local frameworks and strategic plans for DRR, recovery, and resilience building at both national and State levels. These frameworks will be informed by a mix of both tested and innovative technologies and approaches to increase the resilience of communities. All of these frameworks can support bottom-up advocacy at the national and State level, particularly for DRR. Lastly, the EDA

Programmes will place on strong emphasis on engaging with private sector actors throughout implementation, by inviting private actors to conferences, workshops and consultations to demonstrate the operational and financial sustainability of investments implemented as part of the sub-grants.

## Overall contribution to climate-resilience

Overall: The Programme aims to build the adaptive capacity of LGAs and communities in FSM to climate change by developing new capacity, plans, and infrastructure (green, grey, and blue) for improving DRR and recovery. The proposed activities are designed to be replicable at scale across the country. The specific outputs and pathways of the activities and approaches are also highly replicable and scalable, as identified above, which builds the resilience of communities in FSM against climate change impacts.

The Programme will contribute significantly towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by following the pathways envisioned by the Programmes and sub-grants help to advance the critical idea of local co-development and engagement. The Programme is also expected to make a significant contribution to building climate-resilient development in FSM and is in accordance with identified national strategic goals set out by the GCF SM.
