# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 51 OF 75

## D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 600 words, approximate)

### Vulnerability of the country

According to the latest Global Adaptation Initiative (GAI)-based National Development Strategy (NDS-GAI), FSM is the fourth most vulnerable country to climate change and the 78th least ready country in the world (FSM scored 0.640 on the vulnerability scale and 0.360 on the readiness scale). The FSM GCF Country Programme concluded that at present, none of the FSM islands are prepared for climate change.

As highlighted in Section B.1 above, climate change impacts are expected to severely threaten FSM across all sectors, including agriculture, fisheries, coastal resources, water security, and human health. Extreme weather events such as severe weather events and king tides, and increased temperature. Past inundation, surge and storm events have caused significant damage to infrastructure, buildings, roads, bridges, and other critical facilities in the country's coastal resources and fisheries, agriculture and water resources. Currently, approximately 60% of households in FSM are dependent on subsistence farming for their livelihoods, while the rest depend on fishing as a source of income.

### Economic and social development level of the country

As at 31/03/2014, meeting essential calorific needs required an average of USD 1.84 per adult per day, including both food- and non-food basic needs required on average USD 4.54 per day. At the national level, about 19% of the population is below the poverty line (2013). The FSM GCF Country Programme notes that the index, which indicates the extent to which average adult equivalent expenditures fall short of the poverty lines, is estimated at 78%. This means that the FSM is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world and a least developed country that is highly dependent on COFA funding. The COFA currently funds 80% of State budgets and over 90% of public sector employment. The current COFA funding agreement with the Government of FSM is due to expire, and if not renewed this funding source will be severely curtailed leading to an estimated annual financial shortfall of USD 125 million. The COFA funding agreement is currently under review by the Government of FSM.

The economic development highlights a key need for outside funding to support projects to address impacts from climate change.


---
**Page 54**
---

# Absence of alternative sources of financing

132. ESM funding for climate and disaster finance and technical capacity is extremely constrained, particularly when considering the magnitude of potential climate change impacts. Specifically, a previous capacity assessment for FISM identified that there are significant gaps in national and local level capacity to address climate change at both the State and Local level currently outweighs department budgets and that there is limited capacity in national and State disaster risk management systems. The current proposal seeks to leverage existing resources already leveraged in this space, but the funding amount is still inadequate to meet adaptation needs and further; more than 60% of the funds are “uncommitted” by national decision makers which creates a void for effective and coordinated financing of climate change adaptation projects. This is compounded by the fact that only about USD 1 billion has been identified pipeline of projects and programs (est. USD 1.4 billion) exceeds FISM’s national GDP five-fold [1].

## Need for strengthening institutions

134. Capacity assessment conducted in a number of capacity constraints for FISM. As concluded in the GCF Country Programme, at present none of the FSM States has its required level of adaptive capacity to adapt to the effects of climate change and associated disasters. The lack of adaptive capacity is due to a range of factors including: a) an inability to respond to climate impacts in a timely manner and the wide dispersion of the islands in the FSM which makes it difficult for national decision makers to effectively address climate change; b) limited availability of goods and services, costs of energy and transportation, and scalability and connectivity of markets [2]. Further, existing institutional capacity is also inadequate. The lack of coordination between government agencies and civil society is also inadequate, making progress slow and challenging. While decision-makers’ awareness of climate change is recognized as a key factor in the ability to respond effectively to climate change impacts, there are significant gaps in knowledge across LAs, and ultimately building long-term collaborative problem-solving capacity in FSM.

## Needs of local communities

134. GCF financing will support building capacity and undertaking an on-ground interventions for DRR, food security and water security to address the most pressing needs of FISM’s most vulnerable communities. The grant-facilitated project will empower LAs to implement climate change projects based on the specific needs and priorities of vulnerable communities. This will be achieved by strengthening institutional capacity at the local level through capacity building and technical assistance, and by addressing the institutional and capacity barriers that currently prevent the implementation of locally led action climate to address climate change impacts.

## D.S. Country ownership (max. 508 words, approximately 1 page)

### Existing national climate strategy

134. The FISM National Strategy is aligned with climate change policy frameworks at the national, state and municipal level. At the national level, the Programme complies with the Climate Change Policy [2013], the Climate Change Adaptation Action Plan [2015] and the Disaster Risk Management Policy [2016]. The FISM National Strategy is aligned with the Pacific Regional Climate Change Adaptation Strategy 2014-2020, specifically with Strategic Objective 2 “Promote, facilitate and develop training programs for climate change adaptation and disaster risk management” and Strategic Objective 7: “Reduce and manage the risks associated with more frequent, severe and urgent weather events”. ### Aligning with existing policies such as NDCs, NAPAs, and NAPs

139. Although FSM’s contribution to greenhouse gas emissions has always been minimal, the INDC states the country will continue to implement its National Strategy for Integrated Disaster Risk Management and Climate Change Policy (2015) and the FSM Climate Change Action Plan (2014). The INDC emphasizes the urgency to enhance climate change adaptation capacity in FSM. This is consistent with the national development priorities, including the adoption of new technologies, further demonstrating the relevance of the proposed EDA Programme. At all times, FSM’s National Adaptation Plan is currently under development. A detailed list of national, regional, state and municipal policy initiatives relevant to and aligned with the proposed EDA Programme can be found in Section 7 of the Feasibility Study.

## Role of National Designated Authority and Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

### 141.
In addition to this strategic alignment, the EDA Programme has been co-developed by the SPC and the NDA, with the aim of ensuring that the programme is aligned with national priorities and needs. The NDA will be responsible for engaging with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders in the design and implementation of the EDA Programme.

### 142.
The envisaged GCF budget for the implementation of prioritized and identified pipeline of sub-grant projects is USD 19,591,516. This amount includes both the grant funding provided by the GCF and co-financing from other sources.

- The total project budget is USD 19,591,516, all of which is to be provided by the GCF in the form of a grant.
- The program includes USD 13,810,281 as co-finance, and that USD 2,318,571 from the FSM Government and USD 2,462,664 from USAID.

### 143.
The GCF support in form of grant is crucial to address substantial adaptation needs of the FSM communities. The programme will provide a platform for the implementation of a range of climate-resilient development interventions for increased climate resilience of communities and livelihoods. The aim is to initiate a process that will lead to the identification, prioritization, and scaling-up of adaptation solutions in the long-term.

### 144.
Furthermore, the request for grant financing is justified by (i) the needs of the recipients, who are local community-based organizations and civil society organizations; (ii) the limited resources available at the FSM Government and States to leverage other sources of financing. Section B.1 of this Funding Proposal and Section 2 of the Financial Management Plan provide a detailed description of the methodology used for estimating costs and estimates for climate change adaptation solutions far exceed allocated budgets for both LAs and States. This highlights the need for additional funding from the GCF to support the implementation of projects in FSM communities. Further, similar mechanisms from other donors including the Adaptation Fund,150 GEF Small Grants Programme, and USAID/PAACAM have been found to be effective and efficient at supporting local and community groups in developing projects and delivering local climate change outcomes, including in DRR, ecosystem-based adaptation, food security, and water security.

### 145.
Given that the majority of the proposed sub-grant projects are small-scale projects that use grant funding and therefore do not generate any revenues, a financial analysis is largely irrelevant. Given this, a focus has been put on the identification of co-benefits for each project, which will be used to justify the inclusion of these projects within the classical definition of public goods (non-rivalrous and non-excludable).

### 146.
The proposed projects will provide stakeholders with a range of co-beneficial environmental, social, and economic benefits and co-benefits, some of which include:

- **Strengthening local stakeholder identities:** to identify, develop, and implement tailored and focused adaptation measures and needs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 55 OF 75

### Efficiency and effectiveness of the project

147. An ex-ante economic analysis has been performed to assess the incremental adaptation benefits to sea level rise for communities. The economic cost-benefit analysis uses a cash flow model over a 20-year period, with a 3% discount rate. The analysis is based on the results from the baseline scenario and the project as well, as the monetized revenues from resulting externalities such as avoided losses. The approach undertaken is focused on identifying the most probable interventions that will reflect the proposed adaptation needs.

148. The incremental economic benefits (IEB) of the PACC EBMF are positive at USD 39,268,605 and the programme-level EBR is 11%. The programme level EBR is above 6% discount rate and therefore the analysis is considered to be robust. It should also be noted that the analysis is based on a worst-case scenario with 30% of non-investment budget costs for which no direct benefits are envisaged. It is also noteworthy that the analysis does not include any assumptions and nor are benefits discounted.

149. The results from this analysis are conservative, as it was not possible to account for all potential co-benefits. However, these benefits would nonetheless occur under the proposed interventions. Some of benefits would include avoided coastal erosion, avoided crops damage due to increased salinity, reduced water and sanitation costs, increased food production and poverty reduction.

150. For monetization purposes, the analysis is tailored and focused adaptation measures and needs, and health benefits resulting from increased water security. For more details see Annex 2.

### According to the EDA Programme

151. The EDA Programme will demonstrate a strong cost effectiveness and economic efficiency in implementing sub-grants through this EDA Programme. This is achieved by focusing on the Programme's objectives while minimizing transaction costs, in particular given the high complexity of targeting all municipalities in the Pacific region, including those located in remote areas such as outer islands. Indeed, the long distances, at times unfavourable weather, logistics and high-cost challenges of inter-island transportation are significant.

152. Summarizing the comparable costs per beneficiary for sub-projects of the PACC grant mechanism presents the following:

| Country | Cost per direct beneficiary: USD 4,000 |
| --- | --- |
| Cook Islands | cost per direct beneficiary: USD 8,615 |
| Samoa | cost per direct beneficiary: USD 328 |
| Vanuatu | cost per direct beneficiary: USD 2,291 |

### Based on an estimate of the $4.3 billion target amount through the implementation of sub-grants

153. The EDA Programme is estimated to cost approximately USD 197,063,707. The cost per direct beneficiary is approximately USD 363. The EDA Programme compares favourably against these benchmarks and is well below comparable sub-projects in the PACC grant mechanism.

### Application of best practices

154. The programme has identified several potential interventions that would most effectively and efficiently maximize adaptation benefits (See table 4). The interventions were identified and prioritized based on stakeholder consultations. Interventions include proven sound technologies and practices completely aligned with the FSM context


---
**Page 58**
---

153. The focus of this will be on leveraging best available technology and approaches for the DWR, water security, and food security activities including but not limited to cyclone proofing, rainwater catchment systems, endemic vector control, and other relevant technologies as identified in the EDA model and as outlined in the scope of this proposal. Further, given the focused effort to tailor technologies and approaches to the priorities and needs of the LAs and given that the Programme is “Focused on”, it is expected that additional innovation and contributions to the fields will be made through the Programme.

154. The EDA model from this Programme has the strong potential to catalyse additional financing for FSM and its LAAs, as well as other related activities. This is due in part to the Programme’s track record of delivering a fundable track record of project execution, and crucial capacity/experience showcasing effective project and financial management, which will build donor confidence, reduce investment risk, and ultimately unlock future projects and investment for FSM and the LAAs.

## E.5 Activities

All project activities should be listed here with a description and sub-activities. Significant deliverables should be reflected in the implementation timeline. Add rows as needed.

| Activity | Description | Deliverables |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Output 1: Build technical expertise to identify and prioritize adaptation solutions |  |  |

### Sub-Activity 5.1.1 Develop climate adaptation decision support training curriculum including case studies for government officials

An interactive, targeted training curriculum will be developed and tested in a pilot region to identify barriers for the uptake of climate adaptation by local authorities.

Capacity gap assessment of LCA staff and other relevant stakeholders on tools and documents required for climate adaptation with case studies and development of tools and documentation framework.

### Sub-Activity 5.1.2 Climate adaptation decision support training for local authorities

A curriculum will be developed under the guidance of the EDA Grand Facility to train local authorities in the use of the EDA Grand Facility, the process, eligibility criteria, and application procedures.

Training materials on the EDA Grand Facility will be made available for funding updated onto portal.

Training workshops for local authorities (150) and other relevant agencies (85; municipalities (140)

## Improvements of EOs to meet criteria

### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**Programme Monitoring**

155. In its role as Accredited Entity, SPC will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the agreement signed between SPC and the GCF. SPC will be responsible for project-level MEL and reporting in conjunction with the EGU, ESD, and SPDC. The EGU, ESD, and SPDC will work closely with the Education and Learning (SPL) Team and the ECU to implement tools and methods to monitor, evaluate and learn from the project activities. Monitoring of SPC’s MEL will be conducted in accordance with SPC’s Performance Management Plan and during the project’s inception phase.

156. A Monitoring Lead (ML) (a part-time independent national consultant) will be based in the ECU to support MEL across the programme. This will include establishing MEL systems that are aligned with GCF, NGO and other donor requirements. The ML will also provide technical assistance to SPC staff on the use of GCF MEL tools, approaches and reporting arrangements for each of the sub-grants. This will include annual performance monitoring reports (PMRs) and semi-annual progress reports (SPRs). The PMR and SPRs will be developed in collaboration with SPC’s EGU, ESD and SPDC. The PMRs will include capacity development in MEL with a focus on how this can be used to maximize grant outcomes while building the evidence base for the results and impact of the initiative.

157. Results indicators (RI) and performance measures (PMs) will be monitored in accordance with SPC’s Planning, Evaluation, Accountability, Reflection and Learning (PEARL) policy (See Annex 4 of the ESM). The PMRs will include a summary of the RI and PMs for each sub-grant, as well as an evaluation of the effectiveness of SPC’s corporate level monitoring. Monitoring results shall be recorded in the SPRS Results Matrix and will be used to learn from these experiences and inform future project design, implementation, administration and integration with SPC and the EDA Programme itself. Monitoring will enable SPC to make adjustments to its MEL systems as required during the project life cycle. The PMRs will also include a summary of administration and integration within SPC and the EDA Programme itself.

158. For the individual sub-grants awarded through the EDA Facility, regular MEL will be the responsibility of SPC in conjunction with the EGU, ESD and SPDC. This will involve quarterly SPRs that will include a summary of updated information on RIs and risks, and this information will be reported to SPC and GCF. MEL of sub-grants will be supported by EGU and undertaken in accordance with SPC’s PEARL policy.

159. For the individual sub-grants awarded through the ESM Facility, regular MEL will be conducted and certified by SPC to ensure identified risks have been mitigated and that the ESM is being followed appropriately. As per SPC’s SER selection criteria, quarterly SPRs and semi-annual results shall be monitored for SPC to verify if the programme is effectively implemented as approved.

**Programme Evaluation**


---
**Page 70**
---

180\. The independent evaluation exercises will be carried out as part of the MEL Framework under the proposed Programme (please see Annex 11 for cost information):

• Current project progress and results are being completed (to be carried out by ECU and SPC CRU as a self-assessment) as part of activity 2.3.2

• A National MEL expert will conduct evaluations of sub-projects with a view to update the catalogue of adaptation interventions in FSM, as well as to provide recommendations for scaling up or replication.

The MEL expert will be joined by a national ESS and GESI expert to report on environmental, social and gender impacts of the project.

• An external pool of thematic experts will carry out expert analyses and assessments as relevant to the sub-project implementation. The thematic experts will also provide recommendations to the National Assessments Unit 1 to ensure the adequacy and efficacy of adaptation interventions, as part of activity 2.3.3

• A MEL evaluation will be carried out by an independent consultant four years after the start of the project implementation.

• Final project evaluation is to be carried out by an independent consultant and to be completed within 6 months after the end of project implementation

181\. The CFU and SPC Team will be jointly responsible for coordinating the independent interim and final evaluations. The evaluations will be conducted using a question-driven approach, and may include assessments against the MEL Framework indicators. The evaluation findings are expected to be instrumental in contributing – through operational and strategic recommendations – to improve implementation, setting up of monitoring systems, scaling-up of adaptation interventions, and replication of successful projects; and identifying relevant lessons learned for stakeholders in FSM as well as the broader Pacific region. The final evaluations will also assess the extent to which the project has achieved its objectives, including the replicability and scalability of results, differential impacts and lessons learned. The evaluation should also assess the extent to which the project has contributed to building capacity at national level to respond effectively to climate change in FSM. Both evaluations will contribute to the evidence base for adaptation to climate change in FSM and across the Pacific region, and will be published on the SPC website and other relevant platforms

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk from Lack of Mitigation Measures (max. 3 pages)

### Limited interest or engagement from Local Authorities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | High |

**Description**

Limited interest and engagement from LA's leads to underdeveloped project pipeline and limited impact for the EOA Facility and EOA Programme.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Local authorities have been directly included in the development of the Programme proposal to date, and through participatory governance, focused outreach, engagement, and delivered benefits through trainings and project development. The Programme has also engaged with local communities and stakeholders.

### Geographic remoteness of certain Local Authorities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | High |

**Description**

Some LAs are located in outer slots that are very far from main islands/states. Transportation to these remote LAs is a significant challenge. Under the Infragrants, LA's will be engaged in the development of sub-grants for their respective communities. This engagement will communicate project needs based on the relative geographic isolation of their community.

### Local Authorities have insufficient capacity and resources to implement priority projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | High |

**Description**

LA's have insufficient capacity and resources to implement priority projects which results in stranded or underperforming physical infrastructure results for local communities.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Existing capacity for LA is quite limited which is why the EOA Programme deliberately builds multi-stage training for LAs on project development, implementation, and project management as well as sustained technical support guidance, and oversight from project support bodies. In the event a LA needs additional support beyond this in order to become more capable of implementing priority projects, the EOA Programme would consider providing either (i) co-grant development/implementation; or (ii) the EOA Fund would serve as the financial and procurement provider and directly becoming involved with project implementation.

### Local communities and individual households don't support or engage with local priority projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

**Description**

Local communities and individual households do not support or engage with local priority projects stemming from misaligned project objectives and priorities and limited stakeholder engagement and co-development.


---
**Page 72**
---

# Mitigation Strategy

The core principle of the EDA Programme is co-development of adaptation projects tailored to local needs and priorities.

## Limitations

### Limited Local Data and Information

* **Category:** Technical and operational
* **Probability:** Medium
* **Impact:** Medium

Description:
Localized data and information are insufficient to help support development of priority projects leading to underdeveloped projects, sub-optimal design, sting, and implementation of sub-grant activities, and ultimately limited impact.

### Difficulty in Sourcing Technology and Other Inputs for Programme Activities

* **Category:** Technical and operational
* **Probability:** Medium
* **Impact:** Medium

Description:
Provision for the EDA Programme in FIM is complicated given the diverse, dispersed geographies of FSM as well as constrained supply chains from COVID-19 aftermath.

### Existing Capacity for LA on Climate Change, Project Development, and Project Management

* **Category:** Technical and operational
* **Probability:** Medium
* **Impact:** Medium

Description:
Depending on the timeline of the Programme, stakeholder engagement could still be constrained by COVID-19 and the resulting shifts in staff and protocols.

## Mitigation Measures

### The EDA Programme has been adaptive and proactive in finding pathways to engage with needed stakeholders at the local level during the COVID-19 pandemic. By leveraging highly effective national partners and local networks, this foundation will help the Programme to proactively plan for alternatives pathways for stakeholder engagement.

### GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

**162. In the development of this funding proposal and its accompanying feasibility study, an initial risk assessment for the potential priority adaptation measures has been conducted. In general, all of the sub-grant activities are expected to be environmentally and socially sustainable. The overall Programme is being classified as Category-i according to GCF’s Environmental and Social Policy.**

##### Environmental risks

- **Overdrafting of aquifers due to insufficient recharge time**
- **Impact on local water resources due to pumping**
- **Impact on local and migratory fish stocks and marine environment due to infrastructure projects**
- **Environmental degradation due to new building constructions**
- **Noise and air pollution**

##### Social risks

- **Creation of electronic and hazardous waste**
- **Poverty**
- **Community aversion of sub-grants**
- **Exploitation of vulnerable groups in newly created income opportunities and decision-making processes**
- **Risks relating to working conditions (construction)**
- **Impact on local communities**

**164. To ensure the compliance of sub-grants with best practices in environmental and social protection and risk mitigation, GCF will require that each sub-grant activity sets out all policies and procedures relating to the environmental and social management of GCF’s interventions in the implementation of its projects and Programme activities. The Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) and/or ESMS’ equivalent system (EEMS) is to be used to screen and appraise its activities.**

**165. Risk assessment for the proposed project will be conducted by the LAGs to enable them to undertake required EIA and develop ESMP and ESMS as per GCF’s requirements. Annex 6 of this Funding Proposal lays out a set of proposed mitigation measures for the potential E&S risks elaborated above, including provisions to assess adaptation measures using the SREP/ESMS tool.**

**167. The Sub-grant intake form includes exclusionary provisions for social risk activities, which lists all activities where GCF will not provide funding. These include but are not limited to: Community-based Social Protection Assessment Regulators and GCF’s SREP/SER Policy. The combination of small-scale interventions, tight controls and stringent monitoring and evaluation mechanisms will ensure that the Programme is implemented in a socially and environmentally responsible manner.**

**DISCLOSURE**

**168. As part of the commitment to inclusive decision-making, GCF recognizes the right of people to seek, receive and impart information about SPC’s Programmes that have the potential to affect their livelihoods and ways of life. The Programme will provide a report on its activities and impact in the form of an annual progress report for a project. Where SPC commissions such a report, and where not restricted by its partners’ or development partner’s confidentiality agreements, SPC will:**

- **publish the relevant report on SPC’s website;**
- **ensure that copies of the report are accessible to affected persons;** and
- **provide copies of the report free of charge in line with their requirements under any funding agreement.**

**169. In the case of projects that are considered to be medium or higher risk for environmental or social impact, SPC will make the report publicly available at least 30 days before SPC or any relevant development partner makes a decision on whether to fund the project.**

**GREIVANCE RESPONSE Mechanism: Regarding any concerns or grievances from any affected community about the environmental and/or social performance of any SPC project. At any time, an affected member of a community may raise their concerns by lodging a complaint through GCF’s website or to the EDA Programme Board. In addition, SPC will provide information on how to lodge complaints and will ensure that there is access for the affected community to provide feedback, or raise potential concerns or grievances when the ESMS screening process determines it is appropriate. These mechanisms will be accessible and relevant to the affected communities.**

### 18.4 Financial Management and Reporting

All financial management functions remain in compliance with the FCM Financial Management Regulations. This will support the ongoing sustainability of the programme as it will already be operating within the framework of laws and regulations that will govern the management of the programme and implementation after the GCF Fund closes.

#### 18.4.1 SPC’s Financial Management Information System (FMIS)

SPC’s Financial Management Information System (FMIS) is a Microsoft Dynamics NAV-based enterprise resource planning system that provides an integrated solution for financial, human resources, and inventory management functions. FMIS improves the flow of financial information, supports financial monitoring and reporting, and enables SPC to manage its financial operations more efficiently by providing organization-wide separation of income and expenditure by donor and project so that financial and other data can be measured against agreed targets.

As mentioned in Section 8.4., SPC as the Accredited Entity to the GCF will have overall responsibility for quality assurance, including ensuring that all sub-grantees are also accredited entities under the GCF’s Financial Management Information System (FMIS) requirements. SPC will be responsible for the financial execution of GCF funds according to SPC rules and regulations mainly concerning procurement, budgeting, reporting, and monitoring and evaluation (M&E), as well as other aspects of procurement.

During implementation, SPC will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures. This may include monitoring missions, spot checks and participation at relevant meetings. The project will be subject to an annual external audit and internal audit functions with oversight provided by SPC’s independent Audit and Review Committee.

#### 18.4.2 SPC’s FMIS

SPC’s FMIS will be responsible for oversight of project management and financial monitoring, comprising a GCF Project Management Information System (PMIS) and an SPC Financial Management Information System (FMIS). The project will interact with and support the work of the ECU (based within SPC’s MRD), which includes a Programme Coordinator and other relevant staff.

The financial and operational management of the sub-grants provided through the RCSPF will be undertaken in line with the provisions of the Operations Manual (please see Annex 21). Disbursement of funds to successful applicants will be made on the basis of the approved budget, as set out in the SPC’s Financial Management Information System (FMIS) and Finance Policy: Grants and Sub-delegations (2020) and Finance Policy: Anti-Money Laundering and Counter-Terrorism Financing (AML/CFT) (2019). No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s)

Government of the Federated States of Micronesia

Department of Finance & Administration

PO Box PS158 Palikir, Pohnpei 96941

Office of the Secretary


Date: September 07, 2021

To: The Green Climate Fund ("GCF")

Seoul International Center

175 Art center-daro

Yeouido-gu Seoul 12304

Republic of Korea

Re: Funding proposal for GCF by SPC regarding the "Climate change adaptation solutions for local authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia" programme


Dear Executive Director Yannick Glemare,

We refer to the programme titled Climate change adaptation solutions for local authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Pacific Community (SPC) to us on 7 September 2021.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative, the Honorable Secretary Eugene Amor, the National Designated Authority of FSM.


Pursuant to GCF decision B.8(III), the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby provide our no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of FSM has no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of FSM;

(c) In so far as there are any environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.


Kind regards,

Eugene Amor

Secretary, National Designated Authority

Federated States of Micronesia

# Programme background

## The Federated States of Micronesia (FSM)

The Federated States of Micronesia is a widely-dispersed archipelago located in the northwestern Pacific Ocean, comprising four states: Chuuk, Kosrae, Pohnpei and Yap. It comprises 687 islands, of which 74 are inhabited. The distance between the eastern-most state (Kosrae) and the western-most state (Yap) is 2,700 kilometres.

Under the 1979 Constitution, FSM has four states with their own governments, each having its own capital city. The number of inhabitants in each state varies from a population density as low as one person per square kilometre on Pohnpei to more than 35 people per square kilometre on Yap. The largest state is Chuuk, Kosrae and Pohnpei, and Yap are relatively autonomous. Each State has its own governor, judiciary system, police force and other public services.

The FSM is a small island nation with a total land area of approximately 702 km². The FSM estimated population is 113,815, with an annual GDP of USD 129 million and a GPP per capita of USD 1,460 in 2018.

The FSM has a tropical climate with high humidity throughout the year. Most of the coastal regions of the high islands, with more than half the population living in rural areas.

### The Pacific Catastrophe Risk and Financing Initiative (PCRFI)

PCRFI estimates that it is likely that FSM will experience at least one major hurricane or typhoon per year during the next 50 years. In the coming 50 years, FSM has a 50 per cent chance of experiencing natural disaster losses exceeding USD 1 billion.

The projected economic impact of such an event would be catastrophic: a 10 per cent chance of experiencing a loss exceeding USD 470 million and casualties of more than 600 people. The most likely scenario is that the loss would exceed USD 250 million, with a high productivity loss to GDP, while higher spending on post-disaster rehabilitation and reconstruction of damaged infrastructure would lead to higher fiscal deficit and public debt.

The PCRFI project will provide technical assistance for disaster risk reduction (DRR) in communities to the impacts of extreme climatic events as well as long-term water and food insecurity in a context of climate change. The project will be implemented by strengthening the adaptive capacity of local authorities and communities to improve disaster risk reduction (DRR) and coastal management as well as food and water security for the most vulnerable populations.

### The EDA grant facility

The EDA grant facility created under this project and named the Resilient Communities Grant Facility (RCGF) will directly contribute to increasing the adaptive capacity of local communities, particularly women and youth, in FSM. The RCGF will be implemented with gender co-benefits. The project is anticipated to directly benefit 45,010 people, accounting for 40 per cent of the total population of FSM. The indirect benefits are expected to reach up to 98,237 and are projected to be 100 per cent of the population.

### Climate objective

According to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN), FSM is in the fourth most vulnerable country for climate change and the seventy-eighth in terms of climate change readiness in the world. FSM scored 0.649 on the vulnerability scale and 0.358 on the readiness scale, placing it among the least prepared countries to adapt to climate change.

The FSM has a 'high' level of adaptive capacity required to ensure adaptation to the effects of climate change. The country is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO). The country has two seasons: a dry season from November to April and a wet season from May to October. Rainfall throughout the region and country is affected by the movement of the Inter-tropical Convergence Zone.

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Structured to address barriers to response to projected climate impacts by local authorities (LAs) in FSM, with a focus on identifying and preparing the project pipeline while Component 2 is aimed at establishing the Financial flows and access through the creation of the Renewable Energy Grant Facility (REGF) to support the protection and resilience of vulnerable communities.

### Component 1: Local authorities empowered to deliver climate change adaptation services to their populations and ecosystems

Under this component, the accredited entity (AE) will develop an adaptation measure prioritization framework, climate risk screening tools and a training curriculum informed by scientific evidence on projected climate impacts, vulnerability assessments, climate change adaptation risks and identify appropriate adaptation measures. In addition, activities under this component include the development of a national-level adaptation strategy, the development and training curriculum mostly targeting government officials at the local level; SPC will train local authorities (LAs) on the framework and resources identified and provide an introduction to the

This conclusion is based on analysis of daily rainfall data from a subset of Coupled Model Intercomparison Project 5 model simulations. Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, Bureau of Meteorology and Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation (CSIRO) (2011).


---
**Page 84**
---

enhancing direct access (EDA) facility to familiarize local government officials with the objectives of the facility, the process, eligibility criteria, and details of the three thematic areas for adaptation sub-grants. The AE will develop a train-the-trainer’s module and conduct training for national facilitators and local government officials. These trained personnel will then become Facilitating Agents, who will then be responsible for supporting the LAs to prioritize adaptation actions for the facility and train LAs on how to develop adaptation sub-grants.

- Local Government Authorities (LGAs) will also be supported under this component, and will include learning exchanges with other LGAs in the region. The EDA facility will provide a project template that will include sex-disaggregated data at the municipal and subproject level on the three thematic areas and on key socioeconomic indicators to inform the design of future adaptation interventions.

**Component 2: Priority project implementation (establishment of the Resilient Communities Grant Facility) through local community resilience. ECF USD 943,717; SPV USD 605,750 FSM USD 843,571**

Under this component, the AE will establish a Resilient Communities Grants Facility (RCGF) to address the long-term vulnerabilities identified in the funding proposal. Under this output, the RCGF will provide grants for priority projects that are based on the project proposals submitted by the LAs. It will be established and functions as a grants funding mechanism to be governed by the SPC Implementation Unit. The RCGF will be funded through the EDA funding proposal. The RCGF will provide sub-grants for prioritized activities identified under Component 1, which will be implemented in collaboration with the LAs and State Governments across all four FSM States, including outer atolls. It will support 30–40 sub-grants worth USD 75,000–USD 100,000 each. An estimated breakdown of size of projects is provided in Table 1 below.

| Number of Projects | Sub-grant amount (USD per project) | Total average amount (USD) | Municipal/State implemented |
|---|---|---|---|
| 15–20 | 75,000–250,000 | 2,550,000 | Municipal |
| 21–30 | 250,000–500,000 | 7,500,000 | Municipal and State |
| Totals: 30–40 | 500,000–1,000,000 | 3,750,000 | State |

In addition to building climate resilience, the EDA facility, through the awarded grants, will assist in strengthening the local managerial and financial as well as monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) capacity of LAs. This will ensure that the LAs are better equipped to develop adaptation sub-grants and sustain the country’s climate-resilient development in the long term.

The robust governance of this project, which includes a set of steps for proposal selection, ensures that only projects with high potential for impact, sustainability, and well aligned with the GCF investment criteria will be financed by the facility. The project selection process is divided into five stages: (i) pre-screening of proposals; (ii) reviewing and shortlisting the projects by the SPC Implementation Unit; (iii) invitations to the LAs to submit full project proposals; (iv) review and evaluation of the full project proposal preparation; (v) screening of the funding proposals by the Grants Technical Evaluation Committee; (vi) selection of the final list of projects for funding based on the quality of the proposals; and (v) presenting the projects to the Board for approval. The expression of interest process will allow the project management unit to pre-screen sub-grants against the GCF investment criteria, including but not limited to: project alignment with national priorities and social risk level, climate reasoning and rationale, and level of community input/engagement.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

In terms of the adaptation cost indicator, the project has significant present and national level, as it will reach direct beneficiaries (vulnerable states) around 40 per cent of the entire country population. Moreover, the intervention has a widespread scope; all of the country's 75 municipalities will be targeted by the RCGF-supported subprojects, including those located in remote areas. The number of households and individuals that will be directly supported by the RCGF will be determined at the implementation stage, estimates for the number of beneficiaries are currently not available.

It is assumed that at least 80 per cent of the local communities and local authorities (LAs) will be targeted for different subprojects. The assumption reflects realistic experience on the SPM and FCA in this type of locally-focused interventions.

The RCGF will provide grants to subprojects that address at the local level DRR and coastal protection adaptation measures. These actions aim at addressing the impacts of a changing climate, including sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and more relevant of the subprojects financed by the RCGF will be appraised by the SPC project management team (PMT) in terms of their cost-effectiveness. The financial support from financing will support the total or incremental cost of climate interventions and that the financial resources provided focus on cost-efficiency in achieving climate impacts at the local level.

The overall objective is to ensure that the RCGF-supported subprojects provide a strong sense of ownership to the local authorities and subproject proponents, to ensure that the locally-led intervention supported by the RCGF will be: (i) gender-sensitive; (ii) inclusive with particular attention to the involvement of women, girls and vulnerable groups; (iii) the information provided by locally-focused vulnerability assessments; (iii) attention to the avoidance of a lock-in effect and

# 2.3 Paradigm shift potential

The feasibility study presents a theory of change for the intervention which is logically connected to the project’s impact areas. The theory of change focuses on the GCF pilot and its sub-grants are centred on empowering local communities and States to develop and tailor adaptation strategies that will be implemented in their respective contexts. The macro impact areas of intervention – DRR, food security and water security – are identified by the needs assessment and the project’s focus is on the three main adaptation prevention sectors identified in the FSM national adaptation plans. These are linked with the strengths, weaknesses, opportunities and threats analysis that identified barriers and the opportunities that the project will address.

Moreover, in terms of paradigms shift potential the proposed GEF pilot can:

- Catalyse long-term climate impacts and resources beyond the GCF implementation stage.
- The FSM Government is strongly supporting the project as a means to build capacity within the FSM Government. As part of the sustainability plan, there is a resource mobilization strategy which intends to coordinate long term climate donor efforts (beyond the GCF) into a single platform for the FSM Government.
- Build long-term sustainability: The FSM Government is strongly supporting the project, and as such, it will be an important component in the overall national adaptation implementation stage, both as the ultimate owner of the RGF at the handover phase and as coordinator of a potential donor pool, constituting an open-ended facility, once the GCF funding ends.
- Scale up the proposed approach to build adaptation, resilience and climate prosperity at community level. The project will be implemented in all three provinces of the FSM. Also, the overall approach proposed can be meaningfully replicated in other Pacific SIDS that present similar social, economic and geographical conditions and the presence of a strong national government.
- Create an enabling environment: Component 1 of the intervention hinges significantly on ensuring the necessary enabling environment for the successful design, planning and implementation of the project and in the transfer of knowledge and skills which will be pivotal in the design and implementation of the subprojects; and
- Support local communities to build their capacity. The local communities involved in the project, given the strong bottom-up approach and focus in transferring skills and knowledge.

From a point of view of technological innovation, the solutions described in the feasibility study (DBR measures, rainwater catchment systems, use of endemic species, etc.) are known to be highly innovative and transformative. The proposed solutions are similar to those that are proposed for agriculture and ecosystem management (mangroves) will build on indigenous knowledge. Overall, it is expected that technical knowledge will be generated during execution of the project which will be used in the design and implementation of interventions to identified local constraints. This knowledge will be utilized for selecting appropriate

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: N/A**

The project seeks the maximum possible level of conscientiousness in accordance with FSM recommendations and national constraints and specifications. Although the Government of FSM is taking actions to build a policy and legal framework to enable climate change action, structural barriers to accessing finance remain. Public resources, which are largely dependent on external funding, will be insufficient for achieving the project’s objectives. The meaningful reductions in climate change vulnerability and to improve resilience of LAs and beneficiaries will require additional financing beyond what is currently available or appropriate.

An overall economic analysis of the EDA pilot has been performed and it presents a positive net present value and economic internal rate of return.

In response to the need for long-term funding, a medium-term financing strategy for FSM and its LAs, and a resource mobilization strategy have been developed as an output of Component 2. Furthermore, the Government has also submitted commitment letters outlining its intention to provide additional resources to support the project’s implementation in efforts to fund the facility after its initial capitalization is over. Through its focus on capacity-building and institutional strengthening, the project will contribute to the overall success of project execution, and crucial capacity/experience in effective project and financial management at all levels of government and within the private sector is already relatively high. This can help build donor/investor confidence, reduce investment risk, and ultimately unlock funds previously unavailable for climate change adaptation.

There are no specific risks to crowd-out private sector activities, given the intrinsically public-good nature of the activities that the BCGF will finance. By improving the overall economic environment in FSM, the project has the potential to benefit the FSM population, the project has the potential to favour market creation and local entrepreneurship.


### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Programme background:** This EDA programme intends to strengthen the resilience of populations in FSM against the adverse effects of climate change events through empowering local authorities in delivering climate change adaptation services to strengthen the resilience of their respective communities and establishing a grant facility to support the implementation of adaptation measures. The project will reduce the vulnerability of coastal areas to the risk of flooding that will result in environmental co-benefits of avoided soil loss, improved soil structure, reduced sedimentation, and enhanced biodiversity. It will also improve ecosystem functions and services across various agroecological zones. Social co-benefits include increased access to water resources for drinking and irrigation purposes, improved land use and land distribution assets, as well as a reduced morbidity related to waterborne disease and unsafe drinking water.

**Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments:** The project is categorized as medium level of intermediation (P-2) as it involves financing sub-grant activities. It is expected that the project will have minimal environmental and social (E&S) risks with some having a high likelihood of occurrence. The project has been assessed to be in this categorization and that it is within the E&S risk accreditation level of the AIF. The programme has prepared an environmental and social management system (ESMS) as the safeguards instrument for this proposal


---
**Page 90**
---

### ESS 1: assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The AE conducted an initial risk assessment on the potential priority adaptation measures to be implemented under the programme. These are assessed as generally expected to have limited adverse E&S risks and/or impacts. The potential negative impacts include, but are not limited to, the following:

- **Fisheries**: Some of the proposed sub-projects will involve aquaculture activities that may impact fish stocks and marine environment; erosion and soil degradation due to encroachment as a result of over-exploitation of natural resources; introduction of pollutants and generation of effluents and hazardous waste. Potential social impacts include adverse effects on income, food security, and livelihoods for the communities involved in aquaculture heritage as well as on health and safety of workers.
- **ESMS**: The ESMS provides guidance and presents a framework for the identification and management of E&S risks. Sub-projects will be required to undergo a detailed environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and prepare an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) to mitigate site-specific E&S risks and impacts that will be identified.

### ESS 2: labour and working conditions

Activities under the programme are expected to be carried out in accordance with international standards on occupational health and safety concerns. Furthermore, the programme aims to promote non-discrimination and equal opportunities for all workers by ensuring compliance with labour requirements and best practices including promoting the fundamental rights of workers as guided by the International Labour Organization (ILO) conventions.

### ESS 3: climate change adaptation prevention

The programme will support infrastructure-related activities under the climate-induced DRR and coastal protection sub-programme. These include the construction of buildings, roads, bridges, and other structures that could potentially have localized and temporary impacts on water, air, land quality during construction phase, which will have appropriate mitigation measures.

### ESS 4: community health, safety, and security

The programme essentially aims to support interventions that are expected to improve the overall health status and security of local communities. Nevertheless, minor impacts are expected to be experienced by some individuals as a result of certain climate change adaptation interventions. These include potential for nuisance noise and localized air pollution.

### ESS 5: land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The programme categorically excludes supporting any activity that will result in involuntary resettlement. Further, the programme has established a policy framework to be in place to ensure that the sub-grants do not result in involuntary resettlement. These include having no displacement of households or communities, avoiding settlement risks and exclude such proposals from being financed under the programme.

### ESS 6: biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

Some activities of the programme could potentially lead to loss of marine

# Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and GAP and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

## Gender assessment

The gender assessment provides the context of the enabling environment that will enhance the effectiveness of the project in achieving its objectives, including those related to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in 2004, the


---
**Page 92**
---

United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1989 and the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities in 2006. In 2019, the FSM States drafted gender-based violence (GBV) action plans under the national “Policy on the Elimination of Violence against Women” to address GBV. The Gender Development Plan (GDP) for 2023-2027 further highlights seven specific strategic goals for addressing gender and vulnerable groups as part of country-level development planning, implementation, and monitoring. These goals are focused on gender equality, social justice, and sustainable development in the country. The Gender Development Office is a lead institution and is responsible for gender issues in the country. In the agriculture sector, the GDP has identified gender equality as one of its strategic goals. In the planning and implementation “social and gender equity” with an objective of “Improving farm incomes through increased productivity and access to markets.”

50. The gender assessment was compiled based on a desk review as well as consultations with women’s groups in each of the four States in FSM. It indicates that despite the existence of the existing laws, policies, and programs, there are still significant structural and institutional barriers that consider women’s primary role as homemakers and caregivers to children, the elderly, and the sick. Women also face a number of other gender-based constraints such as lack of household agriculture. This is compounded by barriers to accessing agricultural land, training, credit, and extension services. The lack of access to these resources continues to disadvantage women in adaptation and agriculture continue due to exclusion of women from decision-making processes at all levels. Women-headed households are more likely to live below the basic poverty line, and households that are headed by women are more likely to live in poverty and earn less than male-headed households. Women have less income earning opportunities compared with men. The lack of access to credit and other financial services hinders women face in establishing and sustaining micro and small business ventures. Overall, the number of women who own land is very low. In addition, women are more likely to have higher rates of employment in all fields, including agroforestry. Economic activities in FSM could provide a better livelihood for women if they were given equal access to 20% of men in the labour force are considered skilled, compared to less than 20 per cent of women. Women in FSM are also underrepresented in political and decision-making positions and tend to be significantly underrepresented in civic affairs. It is also concerning that there is a high prevalence of GBV and the vast majority of women who experience abuse do not access formal support services.

51. Almost all FSM households grow crops for consumption, barter and sale. Women play a critical role in agriculture as they are responsible for most of the household food production and growing cash crops for income. There are differences between the kinds of crops traditionally grown by women versus men, as well as differences in the location where of women and men farm. Men tend to grow more cash crops than women. The types of land management change (e.g. saltwater inundation, flooding and coastal erosion, soil fertility, etc.) are most prominent for women farmers. Women’s role in agriculture is also critical because they have demonstrated the differentiated gendered roles, rights and responsibilities in FSM and particularly in the agricultural sector. The GDP has identified gender equality as one of its strategic goals to identify the barriers to equal access and benefit from the GDP intended investment. It has on the other hand documented that women are more vulnerable than men to climate change impacts while promoting the gender equality and women’s empowerment. This will be done through the identification, assessment and planned engagement of women groups and their existing capacity in agriculture and other sectors. The GDP will also ensure that there is a continuous ongoing with the Women Development Office and leveraging the support of the United Nations Entity for Gender Equality and the Empowerment of Women (UN-Women) to ensure that there is an disaggregated collection of key indicators, inclusive consistent of women and other

### Risks

#### 4.3 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

- The project aims to improve food and water security, disaster risk reduction and socioeconomic development by strengthening the adaptive capacity of local authorities (LA) at a cost of USD 16.5 million.
- The Government of FSM and the AE are providing in-kind contribution of USD 2.9 million and USD 0.2 million respectively for the project activities to be implemented under the EDA programme, to implement climate change adaptation activities.
- The FEM is a SIDS. The project activities have high socio-environmental benefits but low cash revenue potential due to the nature of the project. Therefore, the AE is requesting a full grant for this project as little potential for revenue generation is envisioned.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute (low risk)

- SPC, as a direct access AE, will serve as both AE and executing entity for this project.
- Headquartered in New Caledonia, it has been operating in the Pacific over 70 years. Its regional


---
**Page 94**
---

office in FSM has been implementing a number of climate change adaptation projects financed by external donors.

### Programme-specific risks (medium risk)

- **Duplication with other project activities**: There have been a number of past and ongoing projects including a GCF-financed project ("Climate resilient food security for farming households across the Federated States of Micronesia") which tried to address similar climate change adaptation issues in FSM. Given the small size of the country, there is potential to coordinate among implementing agencies to avoid the overlapping of activities and outputs. The analysis results in the economic analysis report will be used as a basis for this coordination.
- **Implementation in remote locations**: The AE identified the geographic remoteness of certain LAs as one of the risks as some are very far from main islands and in remote locations. This also applies to the project implementation. The AE has considered lessons learned derived from the AE's established procurement protocol and experiences that will adjust the transportation costs for the project. The AE will work with the current FSM Government procurement service provider and directly support project implementation in collaboration with the LAs.
- **Land ownership**: The project identified traditional social structures related to land ownership as one of the implementation barriers. As some project interventions may limit access to land, the AE has considered this issue during the economic analysis. The AE clarified that the land ownership issue will be addressed differently in different States of FSM, providing a framework for each State to address the issue and ensuring that it is not an issue in each State. In addition, the programme will comply with FSM government legislation and established practices with regard to land ownership and tenure for sub-grants.
- **Displacement**: The AE has considered displacement as one of the risks within the GCF/EA programme; the economic analysis is based on the most probable interventions that would reflect the risk. The economic benefits are expected from avoided costs related to coastal protection. The economic benefits are expected from the saved cost of water, increased yields from climate resilient crops and avoided loss from coastal management. The analysis results in the economic analysis report will be used as a basis for this consideration.
- **Facility funding**: The AE has considered that there is no need to establish an FSG facility to the Government of FSM after completion of the GCF funding period. It will include a reserve fund for any unforeseen costs and/or additional project activities. However, the Government of FSM is unable to make a commitment to recapitalization of the facility during the GCF funding period or beyond the end of the Project implementation funding. There is limited potential for allocation of taxes and engaging the private sector given the public nature of the project activities.

- **The Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) are not subject to targeted United Nations Security Council (UNSC) resolutions**: The Pacific Community (SPC), as accredited entity (AE), confirms that no individual or entity listed on any UNSC resolution is involved in the project. No individuals or entities listed, will be involved in any manner with the project or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer, or beneficiary. Furthermore, no project activities will be undertaken in any jurisdiction where there are targeted UNSC resolutions.
