# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP169

## Proposal name: Climate change adaptation solutions for Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia

### Accredited entity: Pacific Community (SPC)

#### Country/[ins]: Federated States of Micronesia

##### Programme/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1. Impact potential

The climatology of FSM exhibits strong seasonality and inter-annual variability. While El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) has a strong influence on overall climate in the nation, the total inter-seasonal change is not as significant as the intra-seasonal change.

There are four states: Chuuk, Kosrae, Pohnpei and Yap), which are widely dispersed as an archipelago located in the North Pacific Ocean. The main island of each state is shown on the map below found only 74 of these islands. The easternmost State (Kosrae) is located at a distance of 2,700 kilometers from the western-most State (Yap). The estimated population of FSM is 135,816 people as of 2019. The majority of the population lives on Pohnpei and Yap islands that approximately 10 per cent of the population across FSM falls below the food poverty line, while 41 per cent are considered to be food insecure.

The population is engaged in the subsistence production system involving fisheries, agriculture and livestock raising.

The climatology of FSM exhibits strong seasonality and inter-annual variability. While El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) has a strong influence on overall climate in the nation, the total inter-seasonal change is not as significant as the intra-seasonal change.

There are four seasons with the rainy season occurring from May to October. The general direction of rainfall is from the east and north-east towards the west and south-west. The tropical Convergence Zone (TCZ) passes through FSM during this period, resulting in high rainfall amounts. While the average monthly temperature hardly changes throughout the year, there are significant differences between the eastern and western states. Annual rainfall is around 4,626 mm. In general, the eastern states are much drier than the western States, where high intensity rainfall episodes are common. In sharp contrast, the eastern States are more prone to droughts.

The inter-seasonal variability of climate in FSM has been analyzed using historical observational data exhibits non-linear inter-decadal as well as intra-seasonal temperature change since 1950 across the four states. The decadal rate of change in mean temperature is in the order of +0.2°C per decade, which is significantly higher than the global average. In the eastern State of Yap during the wet season, when a gradual decrease (ie withdrawal) of annual mean as well as a significant increase in inter-seasonal variability has been observed. This is due to a reduction in rainfall during the dry season. Based on evidence and thorough analyses of available data, it is claimed that the increasing trend in temperature coupled with decreasing trends in total rainfall has resulted in greater loss of moisture from the top soil leading to higher evaporation rates. The situation is further exacerbated by the La Niña phase of ENSO. Currently scientific opinion foresees that ENSO-related influences on seasonality will worsen in a warming world, with a much greater influence on adverse climate


---
**Page 100**
---

induced hazards and disasters. Climate change-influenced drought is therefore a great concern for FSM.

4. The rainfall variability is found to be greater in the western States of Yap and its islets compared to eastern States. The western States are experiencing an increase in extreme rainfall leading to increased soil erosion, which results in reduced soil fertility, increased landslides that can erode top-soils and increased sedimentation in the downstream areas of the catchment, which chains off water supply for drinking and livestock rearing.

5. However, a much pronounced flooding effect is found in the eastern States due to increasing sea levels that are caused by a combination of SST and sea level rise (SLR). Between 1993 and 2015, FSM has experienced a disproportionate amount of SLR, ranging from 8 to 17 mm/year. The increase in SLR is attributed to the warming of the ocean surface. This suggests that increased sensitivity of cyclone speed with increasing temperature translates into an approximate 4% increase in storm surge for every degree Celsius rise in SST. There has been higher intensity of cyclonic events and associated coastal floodings across FSM. There is no proper drainage system in most parts of FSM, which makes the situation worse. Local evidence suggests that there have been increased levels of inundation, particularly in the eastern islets.

6. The compounding effects of rising SST and increasing sea level has resulted in much greater vulnerability of the coastal zones due to increased flooding and inundation. Infrastructure such as roads, bridges, buildings, and other facilities are heavily impacted. Transportation and communication between islands become increasingly difficult while transportation of goods and services are severely constrained during such events. The destruction of infrastructure also results in loss of livelihoods for many people. Saltwater inundation is often found to affect aquifer systems, making it difficult to collect non-saline potable water from these sources. This has a direct impact on the availability of safe food insecurity, especially among food impoverished households.

7. Future climate change projection has used models that are validated with observational data. Experiments were conducted using the Representative Pathway 4.5 scenario (applying the Representative Pathway 4.5 scenario, as defined by the Inter-Governmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) in its Fifth Assessment Report). The results show that under droughts, floods and storm surges, exhibit significant increase while extreme events will become more frequent and intense. This is expected to have a major impact on water under climate change, even during a 2011–2040 time frame, adverse impacts will be much magnified. Due to the fact that FSM has a very low rainfall pattern, any reduction in rainfall or projected rainfall, the seasonal withdrawal of rainfall in the dry seasons will aggravate seasonal drought. The continued rise in sea levels and ENSO-induced effects will have much aggravated impact on coastal areas. This is expected to have a major impact on water resources, especially location-specific projects and adaptive activities primarily in the inhabited islands, if not in all the islands as well.

8. The project does not present a portfolio of location-specific projects. Rather it takes advantage of the enhanced direct access (EDA) option to mobilize a significant amount under a proposed EDA facility. This will be used for the design and implementation of location-specific projects to be administered by local authorities (LAs). However, the project took note of a barrier analysis that highlighted the need for capacity building at all levels of government. Local governments and municipalities can enhance their capacities to design and deliver on-the-ground projects.

9. The project has two components. Component 1 deals with empowerment of LAs to deliver climate change adaptation services to their population, while Component 2 focuses on priority project implementation by creating an EDA facility for strengthening local community


---
**Page 101**
---

### Coverage of the project in terms of reaching direct beneficiaries is commendable.

About 47 per cent of the FSM population (53,639 people) will benefit directly. Indirect benefits are likely to be even greater as the capacity building and awareness raising activities of FSM will be based on the initial screening and triangulation through stakeholder consultations, the vulnerability assessment and the multi-hazard risk assessment. The criteria for selection will not be the only criterion for mobilization of DHA grants. The capacity of LAs will also be considered in awarding DHA grants to ensure that the most vulnerable municipalities are among the highly vulnerable municipalities so that they will also have a fair opportunity to participate in the grant application processes and utilize the EDA grant support.

### The available information clearly suggests that the impact potential of the proposed project is high.


#### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: High**

The project identifies the following two components to deliver the intended benefits: (a) Component 1: Local Authorities (LAs) are empowered to deliver climate change adaptation services across population, and (b) Component 2: priority project implementation – EDA facility will be created for LAs to implement priority projects. While both focus on capacity building, Component 1 is to strengthen local-level capacities, including that of local institutions, through capacity development activities. The project will create demand for climate change adaptation services with capacity and the need to address local-level climate change adaptation challenges. The project was conceived as a paradigm shift in the way climate change adaptation services are delivered through delivery of EDA grants to LAs.

The LAs seeking EDA grants are primarily at municipal level and will be selected on the basis of their performance under the FSM EDA grant program. Higher preference will be given to LAs that have proven capacity to deliver local-level activities and to those that exhibit high fiduciary capacity, as well as those with a track record in climate change adaptation. Capacity will not be the primary criteria for selecting likely grant-recipient LAs. However, clear preference will be given to LAs that are already implementing or have demonstrated interest in developing a climate change adaptation project according to specific criteria and following GCF investment criteria. If no appropriate LAs can be identified with sufficient capacity, then valuation will be conducted in accessing adaptation financing through such selection approaches.

To address this, the project management unit will set institutional and technical support to LAs with respect to their capacity building needs for climate change adaptation services and enable them to deliver adaptation services to the most vulnerable sites and people.


#### 14. Implementing a combination of the above two components will not only create demand-driven services but also ensure that the most vulnerable communities in the least developed vulnerable LAs have greater access to much needed finance to administer local level adaptation projects. The project will support LAs to develop their own climate change adaptation plans based on local-level vulnerabilities. The strengthening of institutions' and communities' capacity for climate change adaptation is a key component of this project, which provides a way to a systematic approach to adaptation actions at local levels, representing a shift from an ad hoc approach to sustainable delivery of community-based adaptation processes.


---
**Page 102**
---

15. The EDA grant sub-project delivery is innovative at local level, because it draws on the good practices of GCF and aims to customize them to suit local realities. There are parallels between the proposed grant endorsing body or the GCF Board: the proposed program management structure for the EDA grants will be similar to that of the GCF Board, and the TAPs will be similar to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) to apply the customized criteria for EDA sub-project delivery. The innovative aspects of this approach are two-fold: first, they are innovative at the national level, providing ample opportunities to learn from and replicate in other countries.

16. In addition to the replication potential of sub-project delivery processes, the proposed combination of institutional capacity-building and subsequent assistance with applications for much needed financial support is also innovative. This will be done through the Small Island Developing States (SIDS). The demand-driven structure for accessing financial support, on the basis of a clear understanding of local vulnerabilities and clear identification of adaptation measures to be implemented, is also innovative. It will ensure that project inputs and results will be collated and archived in centralized data portals, which will be a source of updated information for future projects. This will include all sub-projects funded by SIDS and atolls located across the four Federated States of Micronesia. The information database is expected to be available on the EDA website and will also be accessible through the portal that will remain outside the EDA grants programme.

17. Under Component 2, dedicated activity 2.3 is expected to focus on fructifying a resource mobilization mechanism for SIDS, with the aim of recapitalizing the EDA facility and prepare handover of its management. It is also anticipated that additional funding from both public and private sources will be sought through the creation of the SIDS Facility to cover the phase in which a number of LAs could be engaged.

18. It is perceived that EDA grant outputs will be based on the understanding of three key aspects: climate change security and food security. The local-level responses will be based on these aspects, taking into consideration understanding and knowledge of the causes of vulnerabilities and targeted responses. This will help to address drought, cyclone prevailing through mangrove enhancement and the creation of breakwaters; ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) for coastal protection; sustainable use, conservation, and management of small-scale livestock and fisheries, etc. Knowledge of the causes of vulnerabilities and of targeted responses is a key to identifying specific response measures, which will be consistent with the overall objectives of each project; therefore, it focuses on knowledge, while leaving ample scope for learning.

19. The EDA grants programme will also provide opportunities for local actors to facilitate the generation and subsequent dissemination of knowledge through the learning exchanges. There are opportunities for local actors, including communities and institutional actors, to participate in the development of sub-projects. The inclusion of capacity-building activities as well as trainings is likely to help inform the development of future EDA and SIDS Facility projects. This will also provide an opportunity for local actors to be involved in the development of EDA grant sub-projects in other atolls and islands through replication of processes and practices that have been developed under this project. The innovative project delivery due to relatively weak LAs will have lasting impacts in concerning local adaptive actions and capacity-building activities. The combination of web portals, periodic workshops, web portals and periodically organized workshops will further enable cross-learning involving actors representing various municipalities and state governments across FSM.

20. In addition to the above-mentioned aspects, the project will also support community-centric adaptation projects, the budgetary allocation for such activities will be incentivized simultaneously. It is expected to catalyze local and national policy change in view of institutional integration of adaptation. The processes of highlighting local adaptive actions and needs for


---
**Page 103**
---

21. Under Component 1, the capacity of LAs will be built towards a greater understanding of climate change impacts to develop and implement their local-level priority adaptive activities. The steps for building this capacity include vulnerability assessments, adaptation planning, and implementation actions which will also contribute to an enabling environment by incentivizing community ownership, which is critical in ensuring long-term sustainability of the adaptation measures (O&M activities) – these aspects being vital for long-term sustainability of the adaptive actions.

22. Analyses on local (particularly) vulnerability assessments and updated analyses of adaptation planning are expected to be carried out at the national level by the relevant national institutions to incrementally extend adaptive actions. This knowledge will enable institutions to serve the needs of their communities in a more effective manner, as well as provide guidance within the project purview are likely to support bottom-up advocacy at both state and national levels, thereby ensuring long-term sustainability.

23. The overall objective of the project is to build local adaptive capacities by strengthening stakeholders’ capacities, plans and adaptive infrastructure involving greens, grey and blue small-scale infrastructures, which will be more resilient in the face of climate change impacts; generally are no regret options. As a result, they are likely to contribute to incrementally building adaptive capacity at the local level. The project is expected to ensure that planning and O&M procedures are not complicated so they will therefore be sustainable in the long run. The management of these small-scale infrastructures is expected to enable the local institutions to deliver climate-resilient pathways for the island nation.

24. In view of the above discussions, the independent TAP is of the opinion that the paradigm shift potential of the proposed project is high.


### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project relies heavily on USAID grant sub-projects, which are awaiting disbursement. It is therefore important to understand their likely and possible contribution of the project to single Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Since the project has gone through an initial scoping of potential vulnerable municipalities and their climate change-related needs, there exists a potential for the project to contribute towards several SDGs in various combinations. This understanding on adaptation responses may be organized under three broad categories: (1) Disaster risk reduction; (2) Food security; and (3) food security. There will be adaptive responses involving mangrove enrichment, natural resources management, and other related activities that are likely to contribute towards the following SDGs:

Based on these responses, it can be inferred that the proposed project will contribute towards the following SDGs in terms of: (a) SDG 1: No poverty; (b) SDG 2: Zero hunger; (c) SDG 3: Good health and well-being; (d) SDG 4: Clean water and sanitation; and (e) SDG 14: Life below water. In addition, contributions to two other SDGs are most likely in terms of SDG 5: Gender equality and SDG 11: Enhanced environmental sustainability.


#### 3.3 Environmental co-benefits

26. Blue and green infrastructure for achieving disaster risk reduction by means of establishing marine safe havens through breakwaters and mangrove protective barriers, respectively, will contribute to the conservation of biodiversity in terms of biodiversity refuge and conservancy. Slope protection in undulated terrain will tend to reduce and arrest soil erosion, which is likely to improve water quality and reduce pollution load in the marine


---
**Page 104**
---

### Economic co-benefits

Reducing the bio-climate damage burdens that are arising from climate change-induced extreme weather events and strengthening livelihoods will offer the most significant economic co-benefit. The protection infrastructure will help avoid damages that occur during high intensity storms and floods. The water tanks will also reduce soil erosion, land degradation impacts by replenishing top soil moisture during the critical growth stage of crops, thus helping to maintain soil fertility and productivity. The water tanks will also provide drinking water, thereby maintaining physical communication during cyclonic storms and floods, thereby contributing to smooth evacuation processes in case of disasters. The micro-scale water retention activities will help to create employment through a robust production system, while the assistance in establishing a value chain for local products will help producers to gain economically from their activities.

### Social co-benefits

Reducing the risks that emanate from insurged weather events is already beneficial to communities in any country. The structures to be put in place will reduce disaster risks. The restoration of degraded mangroves and creation of water tanks will provide immediate relief in the aftermath of disastrous events, allowing patients to be taken to health care facilities and restoring access to drinking water for people living in coastal areas. These rehabilitation activities will have positive social outcomes in terms of enhanced employment and reduction of poverty.

The water tanks will directly serve potable water, which will have significant health-care benefits. The creation of mangrove forests will provide a habitat for marine life, thereby improving marine fisheries due to mangrove conservation and refuge protection through breakwater creation. The micro-scale water retention ponds will also help in the restoration of degraded lands; the micro-scale water retention ponds to reduce drought effects will tend to contribute directly to attain greater food security, which will have social co-benefit potential.

### Gender-related co-benefits

In any society where community-based DRB efforts are purposely considered, it is often observed that women’s participation in project activities and decision-making processes are generally optimized when women's voice are duly included in project designs. The gender action plan of the TAP will ensure that women’s voices are heard at all levels of the project for advancing the cause of women will consider gender equality as a key principle in all aspects of the project design. Women and men will benefit equally from many of the outcomes highlighted above, e.g., improved access to drinking water, enhanced food security through local production systems and value chains, and inclusion of women in various committees and decision-making bodies will help advance gender concerns.

Based on the analysis that both different types of co-benefits, the independent TAP finds the sustainable development potential of the project is high.


### Needs of the recipient

Scale: High


---
**Page 105**
---

10. As discussed under the section on impact potential, FSM is a country vulnerable to climate change. A combination of highly variable rainfall, the influence of El Niño, the ever-increasing surface, as well as sea surface temperature and rising amplitude of extreme tides that are expected to increase in intensity and frequency due to climate change, create high-intensity extreme weather events such as cyclonic storm surges, droughts and floods. The low-lying island nation is also highly vulnerable to sea-level rise and increased ocean temperatures; the sea make the system highly vulnerable to climate change-induced hazards. FSM’s poor state of readiness for climate change adaptation and its limited capacity to respond to disasters and emergency preparedness FSM is currently ranked 78th least ready across the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative identifies it as the fourth most vulnerable country to climate change. These ranking are based on a combination of factors including disaster risk, vulnerability, and the need for an early response are critical important.

15. In terms of development, FSM is integrated with the global economy; such integration often comes at high cost. The vast majority of the population in FSM is reliant on subsistence agriculture, livestock and fisheries based systems. Consequently, the average household income per capita is USD 204 per year (2013) compared to an average of USD L84 per adult per day in need to meet essential calorific requirements). The subsistence production system is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The FSM economy of FSM is not self-reliant and is highly dependent on Compact of Free Association (COFA) funding. In 2013, the compact provided USD 45 million in assistance and over 90 per cent of its funding is allocated for health and education. The overall financial situation of FSM is precarious as the current COFA funding period will expire in 2023, especially after 2023 when the current COFA financing phase will expire. This indicates that FSM is in dire need of international support to finance its much-needed adaptation-related activities.

16. The current technical and financial capacities of FSM at local, State and national levels are severely inadequate. A view of government capacity to respond to climate change. A study financed by USAID highlights that the total costs for operationalizing climate change adaptation policies and actions at State and local levels for outweighing the budgetary provisions of relevant organizations operating in these areas. The overall financial situation of FSM is precarious as the current COFA funding period will expire in 2023, especially after 2023 when the current COFA financing phase will expire. This indicates that FSM is in dire need of international support to finance its much-needed adaptation-related activities.

17. The study also highlights that there are significant capacity gaps at all levels of government and civil society to address such vulnerabilities. All these have left a significant capacity void as regards making any headway towards climate resilient in FSM.


18. The project aims at strengthening the national capacity, which highlights a number of capacity barriers including institutional barriers. It is found that the FSM State and municipal authorities are not adequately equipped with the necessary skills to effectively plan for effective climate change. In fact, the generic inability to analyze vulnerabilities and respond to them, compounded by lack of coordination, information and adequate human resources, severely constrains the ability of the national government to address climate change-related claims due to the level-of-related-understanding among decision-makers is generally high. Yet, do not have a clear understanding of how climate change will impact their communities and the population. Component 1 of the proposed project aims at transferring the funding, receipt and integration of climate change adaptation projects from the USAID Compact to the national which have a mandate at the national and State levels to deliver adaptation-related services.

1. Adaptation responses at community level work better if incremental fee-for-purpose steps are designed and gradually delivered involving the community-level stakeholders. However, if there is a capacity constraint at community level, generalised adaptation at the grassroots level will be more appropriate. The project will focus on building capacities for implementing local-level adaptations in cooperative manner with communities. It will also support the development of a national investment criteria with an emphasis on training, learning opportunities and capacity-building.

4. Although the project focuses on EDA grant support to LAS and communities, it exhibits high sensitivity to various needs of the recipients at both institutional and community levels across the four States of FSM. The independent TAP therefore rates the needs of the recipients as high.

### 1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The project, along with its deliverables, is in full conformity with climate change policy (2013), and the specific objectives are in alignment with the nationwide integrated disaster risk management plan (IDRM). The national government has committed to the implementation of the latter policy should be highlighted. 1) strategic objective #2 aims to "Promote, facilitate and develop projects that will contribute to climate change adaptation" and 2) strategic objective #7 aims to "Reduce and manage the risks associated with more frequent, severe and unpredictable extreme weather events". The project is in alignment with the national disaster risk management plan (IDRM), which is aligned with the second national communication of FSM to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The project also aligns with the national climate change adaptation action plan against coastal flooding, both of which are aggravated by climate change. The approach of mitigation and adaptation in this project is consistent with the national disaster risk management plan (IDRM) and the community-based adaptation approach of both the climate change policy and second national communication of FSM.

The national government's determined contribution to achieve objectives of the Paris Protocol highlights priority adaptation actions that are in line with the project deliverables. The document also highlights the need for capacity building, technical assistance, planning, management, and operation and maintenance projects, which are at the core of the proposed project. The FSM Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) is aligned with the national disaster risk management plan against climate change in FSM; whereas the project finds great overlaps in approach and delivery.

In addition to having national-level policy alignment, the project is also exhibiting good alignment with State-level action plans. The States of Koror, Palikir, and Yap have developed State-level adaptation action plans that refer to Kora, Koror, Palikir and Yap States. These State-level action plans are focused on climate change adaptation in line with national disaster risk management plan against climate change. It is expected that the EDA grant sub-projects will find inputs from respective LEAPs, verify them through stakeholder engagement and be integrated into EDA sub-projects.

The municipalities of FSM have developed local early action plans (LEAPs) as part of the International Climate Initiative where causes of specific vulnerabilities and adaptation needs are highlighted. It is expected that the EDA grant sub-projects will find inputs from respective LEAPs, verify them through stakeholder engagement and be integrated into EDA sub-projects.


---
**Page 107**
---

This will be an integration of existing policies while also devising and delivering adaptation in all tiers of governance in FSM.

42. The FSM country programme submitted to GCF identifies the need for achieving food and water security, initiatives on DRM and resilient infrastructure and ecosystem strengthening. Therefore, it is proposed that the project should focus on these areas and the lessons learned will be shared with GCF. The same country programme recognizes the difficulties in leveraging private sector financing for adaptation projects. It also highlights the importance of public-private partnerships for financing assistance to advance adaptation activities in FSM. The EDM Grant Facility has already been identified as a potential source of funding for the proposed project, which is an adaptation project. The involvement of LAs in the delivery of adaptation and for providing leadership on climate change issues at all levels of government has been recognized by the country programme and has been part of the country programme, and is the central consideration of the proposed project.

51. The Pacific Community (SPC) is a regional inter-governmental body operating in the Pacific region with its headquarters based in Noumea, New Caledonia. SPC was established in 1947 and has been operational since 1947 and has strongly influenced the Pacific Island's development. The SPC has a long history of working on climate change adaptation issues through the application of its knowledge base, science and technology in addressing cross-cutting issues such as DRM, climate change adaptation, food security, gender equality, management of non-commercial forests, etc. SPC is also the lead executing entity (LE) for the Adaptation Fund entity (AE) to GCF and has already implemented a few small-scale adaptation projects by mobilizing finance from the Adaptation Fund. The SPC has been involved in climate change adaptation even beyond the regional scale in the Pacific. SPC has the right exposure and experiences to deliver adaptation projects at national level, and it is also the lead executing entity (LE) for GCF. SPC has a regional office based in Papeete, which has administered a number of adaptation projects in French Polynesia. The SPC has been involved with the Pacific Plan for scaling up adaptation in the Pacific and small island developing States.

56. The FSM Department of Finance and Administration, the executing entity (EE) and also the national focal point for climate change adaptation in FSM. The EE has successfully worked together with GCF and successfully implemented the support projects. The NDA has been instrumental in designing and implementing the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which is a follow-up to the Framework Programme (FP50). It is expected that the NDA will remain the focal point in providing guidance and decisions on EDM grant awards, while the four states will be fully involved and are expected to provide inputs into the project design. The EE has also been instrumental in ensuring national decision-making body consisting of representatives from key stakeholders will endorse her policy recommendations for climate change adaptation.

As already planned, resources will be mobilized through the NDA and LAs to foster the partnership between the four states and the EE to ensure a high level of ownership and commitment. 41. The SPC has taken the lead to devise the EDA project in cooperation with the NDA. The SPC mobilized strong support from the NDA to engage stakeholders across FSM, including all four States and LAs, in the development of the project proposal. This engagement allowed the NDA and SPC to combine scientific knowledge and awareness of climate change adaptation issues at national level. The EE has also been instrumental in providing inputs into the planning of the EDM grant sub-project together with the expected institutional capacity building.

In view of the above discussions, it appears to the independent TAP that country ownership of the project is high.

1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: High**

The project will be implemented over seven years at an estimated cost of USD 19.71 million, which is equivalent to a total of USD 143.59 million in the form of grants. Considering the nation's status as small island developing State, the request for full concessionality is justified.

The greater part of the project costs are programmed to provision EBRD grant support under an EDA grant window, which accounts for about 71 per cent of the total budget. The co-financing amount is about USD 3.12 million, most of which is provided by the FSM Government and the remaining by the World Bank through a loan facility (see Table 1.6).

As indicated in the earlier sections, there is a dearth of financing for climate change adaptation projects in the country. Currently no private sector financing are available for building resilience in FSM. Since the majority of the project activities will be community-driven, small-scale and incrementally contributing no regret adaptation actions, they will not require significant co-financing from the private sector.

Therefore, the need to mobilize GCF grant financing through the involvement of public sector institutions is critical.

The project presents budgetary allocation that identifies high value for money, as the estimated transaction cost on an island setting is comparable to projects financed by GCF in other countries. The project will also require significant transportation and other costs. Since many of the activities will be accomplished by local communities, it is expected that the transaction costs would be lower than those incurred expatriate support and consultants compared to most GCF financed projects.

The long distances between islands, at times unfavourable weather, logistics and high- cost challenges make it difficult for project beneficiaries to access public services and deliver goods and services. Any analysis of economic performance must take into consideration the context of the project. The estimated cost per beneficiary is USD 345, which is deemed adequate and reasonable in order to achieve the project's objectives while minimizing transaction costs. It should be noted that this figure does not include the municipalities in each of the States in order to include particularly vulnerable populations living in rural areas and outer islands. It is found that the cost of delivery of similar grant programmes in other projects under GCF are higher than USD 345 per beneficiary, but lower than USD 600 per beneficiary under the proposed project (ranging from USD 436 to USD 8415 as against USD 370 for the present project).

The incremental adaptation benefits provide the basis for conducting the economic analysis of the project. The economic benefit cost analysis considers a cash flow model over a 20 year period, with an interest rate of 1 per cent and a discount factor of 9 per cent over a period of 50 years. However, the analyses assume most probable adaptation responses under EDA grant sub-programme in FSM, which are based on past experiences and best practices from past projects. Since actual activities for the EDA grant window cannot be ascertained at this stage, it is not possible to make assumptions about direct benefits.

The overall economic performance of the proposed project under the above assumptions appears effective and economically highly desirable. The economic net present value (NPV) is USD 105 million, which is significantly higher than the amount to be spent in FSM. Moreover, the economic internal rate of return appears to be 11 per cent when compared with a discount factor of 9 per cent over a period of 50 years.

The economic soundness looks even more attractive with the realization that about 30 per cent of the budget is programmed for co-financing and that significant indirect benefits may accrue. However, the inputs for institutional and community-wide capacity-building will eventually result in non-tangible benefits, which are expected to contribute to long-term sustainability as well as replication of adaptation actions on the ground.


---
**Page 109**
---

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The proposed EDA project is a good example of this kind of project and the independent TAP recommends the Board to approve the project.

# Overview

This Gender Analysis and Action Plan (GAAP) provides the overall context and framework for mainstreaming gender into the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project "Climate change adaptation solutions for local authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia." Accordingly, it has been developed in line with the GCF Gender Policy and the GCF Gender Mainstreaming Guide and includes the following sections:

- Overview of the legal and policy framework related to gender in the Federated States of Micronesia
- Analysis of population, household, education, and income data from the 2010 Census and 2013/2014 Household Survey
- Description of income and employment in the agricultural sectors by gender from the FSM Agricultural Sector Profile
- Overview of violence against women issues in FSM
- Overview of the GCF Voluntary National Review of national priorities and progress assessment for reporting purposes
- Comparison of gender indicators for FSM and other Pacific Island Countries
- Assessment of gender mainstreaming gaps and areas
- Recommendations for sub-grants mainstreaming gender
- Description of sub-grant implementation roles and responsibilities with regards to gender mainstreaming
- Overall Gender Action Plan for the sub-grant activities
- Framework for gender mainstreaming in sub-grants

## Methodology

This GAAP was completed through a desk review of FSM's legal and policy framework and publicly available information pertinent to assessing connections with women's groups for each of the four States in FSM were additionally undertaken through November–December 2023. The women's groups/representatives consulted were the following:

- Pohnpei Women’s Council
- Yap Women’s Commission Officer representing Yap women’s groups
- Kosrae Women’s Council and
- Chuuk Women’s Council

The consultation for the Pohnpei Women’s Council was completed in person; however, due to the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic, the remaining consultations were completed via virtual meetings led by the national consultants.

The consultations focused on how gender vulnerabilities manifest within the three thematic areas covered by the proposed EDA programme (disaster risk reduction and coastal protection, food security and water security). The following measures were identified as priority actions to address these vulnerabilities in a gender-sensitive manner. Through the consultations, food security projects and assisting women farmers and gardeners were deemed as priority measures for the EDA programme to reduce gender vulnerabilities in FSM. Please see Annex I for the full summary of women's groups consultation completed.

# Gender Analysis and Assessment

## Legal and Policy Framework

International and Bilateral treaties and agreements that FSM is party to and supports:

* Article X of the Federal Programs and Services Agreement between the Government of the Federated States of Micronesia and the Government of the United States where the US Agency for International Development (USAID) is responsible for providing disaster assistance and coordinating the US Government's response to disasters in FSM.
* Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW).
* Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995, Fourth World Conference on Women).
* The Programme of Action for the Implementation of the 2002 World Summit on Sustainable Development.
* The 2000 Millennium Declaration.
* The 2015 Paris Agreement of the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).
* The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015 – 2030.
* Ratification of the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1993, UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1994 and the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD) in 2016.
* The FSM Government participated as a First Time CEDAW Report in 2018 and the CRC Report in 2020. General observations and recommendations have been tabled to the FSM Government for consideration in follow up actions by the review panel of experts under CEDAW and CRC.

Examples of Recommendations include the following:

* The government should provide a report on the implementation of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.
* Without delay, implement the First CEDAW.
* Implement a national development plan to provide a strategic and coordinated framework for information sharing and collaboration in addressing climate change and climate resilience.
* Create a national development plan to mitigate the effects of climate change and increase funding and resources for emergency preparedness and response facilities as part of the ongoing strengthening of disaster risk management systems.
* Ensure that the National Climate Change Policy considers policies to alleviate the disproportionate impact on women, children, and persons with disabilities.
* Ensure that the National Policy on Climate Change considers the impact of climate change on children and women and the role they should have in the actions implemented in their favour.


---
**Page 116**
---

- Ensure that women, especially rural women, children, persons with disabilities and indigenous and local communities are meaningfully engaged in the development and implementation of climate change and disaster risk reduction frameworks.
- Allow for the establishment of a Department of Environment, Climate Change and Emergency Management (DECEM).
- Convene as a member of the International Labour Organization with the view to ratifying its core conventions and bring labour legislation in line with international standards.
- Improve national data collection by completing a census by 2022 and ensure that disaggregated data is produced on gender, age, disability status, ethnicity, sexual orientation, gender-based violence, universal health coverage, universal access to safe and reproductive health and rights, and empowerment of young people.

Sub-Regional and Regional Agreements

* SDS Accelerated Multi-Year Action (S.A.M.O.A.) Pathway (2014)
* Pacific Climate Change Framework (2014 – 2024), Key Outcome 4 is on Climate Change.
* In June 2020, the FSM Government endorsed the Global Principles of Practice of National Mechanisms for Gender Equality (GPPNME) and the Pacific Regional Framework (PRF).
* Pacific Leaders’ Gender Equality Declaration (2012)
* Jinef Kiped Declaration by the Micronesian Women’s Conference in Republic of Marshall Islands (2017)
* Pacific Platform for Action for Gender Equality and Women’s Human Rights (2017 – 2030) adopted in Davao City during the 13th Triennial Conference of Pacific Women and the 6th Ministers for Women’s Meeting
* Boe Declaration (2018).
* Kapingamarangi Declaration (2019)
* Framework of Resilient Settlements in the Pacific (FRSP)
* Outcomes of the 5th, 6th and 7th Conventions of the 13th Triennial Conference of Pacific Women and the 7th Pacific Ministers for Women Meeting (May 4-2021) focused on Women’s Economic Empowerment, Gender Based Violence and Gender Responsive Climate Justice.

National policies and institutional arrangements

According to the Pacific Women FSM gender situation analysis, the country is in the early stages of implementing a legislative and supporting policy framework to promote gender equality and women's human rights. The government has developed a National Strategic Plan on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). It has a National Strategic Plan on Gender Development and Human Rights (2017-2025) and is in the process of developing national action plans and regional action plans under the national Elimination of Violence Against Women and Girls policy (with support from the United Nations Population Fund). The Pohnpei State Family Protection Act was passed in November 2017 (after a delay due to opposition by some members of parliament) and the Yap State Family Protection Act was passed in 2016. Chuuk and Yap States have yet to pass family protection legislation.*


---
**Page 117**
---

The FSM Strategic Development Plan (2004-2023) further highlights seven specific strategic goals for addressing gender and vulnerable groups as part of country level development.1

* **Strategic Goal 1:** Enhance and promote the cultural, economic, legal, political and social development of women and children throughout their life cycles
* **Strategic Goal 2:** Ensure equal access to education and training opportunities for women
* **Strategic Goal 3:** Mainstream gender issues into decision making, policies and strategic investments
* **Strategic Goal 4:** Maximize women's contribution to and participation in democratic and development processes at all levels of government
* **Strategic Goal 5:** Strengthen the institutional capacity of the women's programs in FSM
* **Strategic Goal 6:** Strengthen the institutional capacity, effectiveness and impact of youth organizations
* **Strategic Goal 7:** Strengthen youth development through social, economic and political participation

The National Gender Policy, endorsed in 2018, has six strategic goals, linked with other gender equality commitments including the Sustainable Development Programme, CEDAW, Pacific Leaders' Declaration on Women's Rights and SDGs.

* **Goal 1:** Better representation of women in decision-making
* **Goal 2:** Elimination of gender-based violence
* **Goal 3:** Gender equality in education
* **Goal 4:** Address barriers facing women in the workforce
* **Goal 5:** Women have better health care and improved choices over their fertility
* **Goal 6:** Women's equal access to land, water and other natural resources

The Policy recognizes climate change and disaster risk reduction (DRR) as priority cross-cutting issues, noting that adaptation can avoid exacerbating existing gender inequalities and vulnerabilities by planning for population displacement and loss of livelihoods. The policy also highlights the importance of FSM Elimination of Violence against Women policy a key outcome of work to date), women in decision making, women's access to land and other natural resources, women's economic empowerment, child protection and gender mainstreaming, with links to other development partner initiatives and programs.

The principal driving policy framework for the issue of food security and agriculture in FSM is the National Agriculture Policy (2015-2030). The policy aims at achieving gender equality outcomes, namely: to improve farm incomes and livelihoods with particular focus on gender and vulnerable groups.2

Additional policy frameworks, laws and programmes supported include:

* **The FSM Constitution Article 4, section 4 ensures equal protection, which guarantees fundamental human rights regardless of gender, sexual orientation, race, ancestry, national origin, religion, language, and disability**
* **FSM Environmental Protection Act (2012)**
* **Kosrae State passed its Family Protection Act in 2014. Pohnpei State passed its Domestic Violence Act in 2017**
* In 2019, Chuuk State Government raised the Age of Consent from age of 13 to 18 (C.S.L No. 6- 85).

In 2019, Pohnpei State Government raised the Age of Consent from age 16 to 18 (S.L. No. L9- 155:19).

* FSM Disability Policy (2009–2016)
* FSM Health Policy (2017–2023)

FSM Nationwide Disaster Risk Management and Climate Change Policy (June 2018). At the state level, there are three disaster risk reduction plans for Chuuk, Kosrae, Pohnpei and Yap States, for Disaster Risk Management and Climate Change. Action plans and policy are linked to the Sendai Framework.

FSM Agriculture Policy 2012–2016 The results of the 2016 Integrated Agriculture Census are being used to update the Agriculture Policy.

* FSM National Plan of Action Ending Violence against Women (EVAW) Policy in 2018. State and National implementation plans of the EVAW policy (2018) are being developed.
* The State Law No.9-43 was passed for the purpose of addressing the sexual harassment issue.
* State Law No.9-63 was passed for the purpose of addressing the special needs and accessibility provisions in public buildings and facilities. State Law No. 9-24 was passed to provide persons with disabilities better access to government facilities, among other purposes.

The FSM Gender Stocktake in 2012 highlighted the following key issues for gender governance and mainstreaming gender into policy and legislative frameworks in FSM:

* Budgets for the national and state programs cover only operational costs, including salary, travel and subsistence. The majority of the budgetary resources are not allocated to projects that fund through funds secured from development partners and the US Federal Funding system.
* Participation of women in decision-making processes is low at all levels within FSM Women continue to be absent or grossly under-represented at the legislative and executive levels of government.
* Historically FSM is a matrilineal society where ancestral descent and land is passed down through maternal tribal lineage. In the modern age however, this historical lineage cannot be generally applied in the context of women’s rights and gender equality. Sub-projects will be required to undertake a gender analysis and EAS screening to ensure these issues are addressed.
* The majority of the budgetary resources are not allocated to projects that fund through funds secured through multiple entryways for stakeholder consultations through the EDA grant mechanism.
* Other barriers to full participation in public decision making and participation at the higher government level include the lack of opportunity to undertake leadership training and the lack of exposure to policy development processes, including the budget process.
* Lack of awareness of the issue of gender equality was highlighted by FSM departments as a major barrier to achieving gender equality in all sectors of the economy.
* Lack of sex disaggregated data to inform policy developments

The Department of Health and Social Affairs hosts the Gender Development Office (GDO) as the focal point for gender mainstreaming, and the CEDAW Committee, with its FSM Gender Stocktake highlighted critical challenges, including:

* GDO is not strategically located within the central government structure to influence the integration of gender across the policy, programming and resource allocation spectra.


---
**Page 119**
---

- GDO has few resources and no process to facilitate the mainstreaming of gender and women’s human rights across the whole of government.
- GDO lacks expertise and experience in gender and human rights analysis and integration, particularly in relation to technical support, gender-related planning and analysis, sub-grant management, information management and dissemination and evaluation
- Further it should be noted that in addition to the GDO, FSM National government funds some gender positions within its various departments. The responsibility for ensuring that women’s and gender equity work in Chuuk and Kosrae is largely the responsibility of CSOs including the state women's umbrella groups.
- Since the adoption of the EDA grant framework in 2018, the Department of Health and Social Affairs and GDO have made significant progress including:
    - Actively engaging in policy, programming and resourcing spectra to incorporate gender equality positions within all government processes (for example the SOGs and the Vulnerable National Review (VNR)).
    - Increased budget allocation for gender equality.
    - The inclusion of gender equality in the National Policy and State Action Plans (action plans not yet endorsed).
    - Making efforts to support women’s economic empowerment, sexual reproductive health rights and ending violence against women
- The Pacific Women Country Plan for FSM highlighted some progress in addressing these gaps, but many of them still remain significant barriers for GDO mainstreaming activities.
- The FSM National government funds the six State Women's councils or associations, umbrella groups for all the women’s groups in the state: Chuuk Woman Council (CWC), Kosrae Women’s Association (KWA), Pohnpei Women’s Council (PWC) and Yap Women’s Association (YWA). These women’s groups have been involved in the development of the FSM National Gender Policy and the six strategic goals in the National Gender Policy as well as implementing projects and programs identified by members. The FSM National government has also established a Women’s Commission, which is made up from an FSM Women's Council. That body was legally established prior to the 2016 conference in Yap. The Women’s Commission was re-established on 3rd October 2018 following the FSM Women’s Conference 2018 and is now the coordinating body or national voice for women; and has received funding support from National government. A draft Gender Action Plan (GAP) in which women have been consulted and a key outcome of that meeting was the endorsement of the FSM Women Council Strategic Plan.
- The VNR notes that “it is in the civil society sector where women have an outsized impact on the collective consciousness and development of the many islands in FSM. More organized support and public-private and private-public partnerships are needed to ensure this.”
- Women’s groups have been consulted in the development of the GAP and will continue to be part of the stakeholder engagement process as they are an integral part of communities across FSM.

## Existing Gender Inequality – Statistical Overview

The poverty profile for FSM highlights that at the national level about one-third of the population lives in female-headed households. The average size of female-headed households is 5.2 compared to 4.8 for male-headed households and are also on average larger and have more children across all states except Yap. Further, at the national level half of female-headed households live in poverty compared to 40% of male-headed households (Table 1).

| Household Type | % of Children under 5 | Average Number of Children under 15 | Poverty Incidence |

In 2013/2014, male-headed households across all states had higher average annual income than female-headed households. At the national level, female-headed households earned $9 less on average than male-headed households. The average annual income for male-headed households in Kosrae earning 2% more than female-headed households compared to a 42% difference in Chuuk. In Pohnpei and Yap, female-headed households earn an average of $100 (USD$35) less per year than male-headed households earnings less than USD $500 (37.4% of male households and 36.7% of female households). The average annual income for FSM is USD$28,900 (SD=USD$30,000) shows higher percentage of male-headed households (10.2% vs. 5.7%) (Table 2).

and female headed households saved 9% more than male headed households.12 While the expenditures breakdowns for male- and female-headed households is pretty similar with food-and non-alcoholic beverages accounting for 34.4% and 34.5% of monthly expenditures, respectively there is a substantial difference in other expenditure categories with male-headed households spending on average 15% more than female-headed households.12

In the workforce, similar disparities exist. Across the States, 67.2% (Chuuk) to 70.3% (Pohnpei) of the male population was employed in private households compared to only 48.9% (Chuuk) to 54.1% (Pohnpei) for females and 48% engaged in home-based work (45% subsistence, 54% market-oriented). For females, only 43.3% (Evaluating the gender gap in employment in the FSM: A review of the literature), 29.7% were employed in public sector jobs and 10.8% worked in private sector jobs. In contrast, 65.5% of males were employed in formal work and 57% engaged in home-based work (58% subsistence, 41% market). Roughly a quarter of the male population was employed in public sector jobs compared to only 9.2% for females. Only 30% of men were employed in fishing compared to just 2% of females and 15% of males were employed in public administration. In terms of income, women earned an average monthly income of $487 compared to $600 for females in FSM as limited compared to those for men; and the income activity available to women tend to be more precarious.

The gender differences in labor force participation are further evident in the FSM Social Security Administration statistics. In 2016, women represented 30% of Social Security contributors in FSM, and on average earned $487 per month compared to $599 for men. Women also earn less than their male counterparts. While this gender earnings gap has reduced substantially over the last 20 years (19% since 1991). A similar trend is evident when comparing women’s and men’s general social security contributions. Out of a total of 35,847 social security contributors were 40% female and the earning was about 14%. For Pohnpei: 18% women and an 8% gap; for Yap: 43% women and an 11% gap; and for Koror: 36% women and 16% gap. The WHS data also shows that in the public sector, which is a major employer of women in the tertiary sector, the FSM Social Security Data shows that women’s participation is the same as men, and in addition to more precarious income activity.

For education, women in FSM have significantly lower educational attainment than men. At the elementary school level 69% of females over years and over in FSM have completed at least elementary school compared to 84% for males. In secondary school, only 30% of females have completed high school while 78% and 90% of males, respectively have completed elementary school compared to only 68.5% and 77.8% of females respectively. At the tertiary level, 42% of males have graduated from college or university compared to just 13% of females. The difference that is evident in basic education in Yap and Koror states: 72.9% and 68.5% of men had graduated high school, respectively compared to 46.1% and 42.1% of women. Women with bachelor’s degrees are also also about the proportion of women holding a master’s degree (Table 3) at the national level (Table 3).

Further the 2012 fertility Monograph in FSM further documents that gender disparity in FSM In 2012, among women aged 15-49 years old, a relatively high rate of teenage fertility. Overall, almost births to women younger than 15 years of age were registered during the period 2005- 2010, and 38% of these were in the 15–19 year-old age group. The census data show that only a small proportion of teenage women who gave birth attended school which creates additional hardships and lack of income potential in the future for the young women.12

In the 2016 Agricultural Census, most of the people aged 15 and over in households with land used for agriculture had not graduated from high school—58% of males and 59% of females. Twenty percent of males and 21% of females reported that they had graduated from high school, and 12% of males and 11% of females had completed a bachelor’s degree.

The FSM Education Indicators Report for 2018/2019 highlights that in 2019, net enrollment in FSM schools is 65% at elementary level, whereas it is only 65% in ECE and 46% Secondary level and while boys’ and girls’ net enrollment rates are similar (73%) at the primary school level, they differ significantly further in secondary level. In grade 1, gross intake rate is slightly higher for male students compared to female students, but this trend reverses by grade 5. The gender gap in grades 6–9 is even more pronounced.

Possible reasons for this variation by gender could be associated with late entry of males in grade 1, whereas higher drop-out rates among females in grades 4 and above. In contrast, the net enrollment rate at primary school survival rates throughout FSM are consistently low with female students having slightly higher survival rates than male students. The data shows roughly—45% (39% Male/50% Female) survival rate of cohort entering grade 1 to grade 2, whereas only about one-third of those entering grade 2 survive to completing Grade 12 is very low at about—(20%: 17% Male/25% Female). These education gaps lead to less opportunity for women and girls in the workforce and in decision-making roles. This is particularly true for the specific project, it indicates that stakeholder engagement needs to be actively aware of and addressing these inequalities and barriers to ensure that women’s voices are heard and their rights respected. Further, women's interest groups, community groups, and civil society might require additional support, capacity development and coaching and mentoring to be able to fully and actively engage in the envisioned climate adaptation project.

Women in Public Life and Decision-Making

Undeniably, women in FSM have been totally absent from the legislative and executive levels of government, and continue to be significantly under-represented in civic affairs (see Figure 1) In-2020.


---
**Page 123**
---

there were three women serving as legislators in the Pohnpei State Legislature, and in Chuuk, there were two female senators.

In the public service, some women have achieved seniority and are able to influence government policies and programs. Statistics show that of all the major formal work sectors (national and state government, private sector, and non-governmental organizations), women hold 10% of the positions in the national government, 25% in the state governments, and 38% in the private sector.

![](https://example.com/image.png)

Figure 1. Women in Political Leadership Positions in FSM States, 2020 Data (Source: VNR Report, 2020)

There are no legal barriers to women's representation in government, but there are significant socio-cultural restrictions. Traditionally, matrilineal societies enable women to actively participate in decision-making processes and leadership roles within the family unit. Women can inherit property and land, and may be male, usually an uncle or brother. The EDA programme will ensure the participation of women in the decision-making process at all levels through the establishment of a Women's Council under the EDA Facility. The active participation of women is embedded in the GBD as well as in the programme’s Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP).

At the local level, women are involved in small businesses, cooperatives, within traditional structures and within faith-based organisations. Such women’s groups are organised and visible at community level. The nature and structure of these groups vary from State to State and from community to community. It is becoming more consistent across different islands, communities and ethnic groups within FSM. There is a Women's Council in each of the four States.

Agricultural Income and Participation

The 2015 FSM Agricultural Census provides a more detailed look at jobs and utilization in the agricultural sector (crop raising, livestock, fisheries, forestry, and hunting) which helps provide additional context for gender disparities. These considerations will need to be balanced by the sub-grants, particularly those that are focused on women.

Just over 90% of households recorded in the Agriculture census had access to land they used for agriculture. Across FSM the household was male in 80% of households. Yap had the highest proportion of female-headed households (25%), followed by Kosrae (17%), Chuuk (16%), and Pohnpei (13%). In these households, 30% of males and 19% of females 15 years old or

### Violence Against Women

In October 2014, FSM conducted a National Family Health and Safety Study (**) which highlighted the major forms of violence against women in FSM including the following significant findings:

- Almost one in three ever-partnered women in the FSM (32.8%) have experienced physical and/or sexual violence by a partner at least once in their lifetime and slightly over 18% of ever-partnered women were victims of intimate partner violence within the last year.
- Nearly one in four ever-partnered women (24.1%) experienced physical and/or sexual violence by a partner in the 12 months preceding the interview.
- Four out of five women who experienced intimate partner violence (81%) were injured at least once in their lifetime due to the violence.
- More than half of the abused women (53%) never told anyone about the violence. Those who did disclose it mostly confided in family members and friends. 89% of ever-abused women never reported the violence to any formal service or authority.
- The most common reason for not seeking support from formal services or authorities was that respondents thought the violence was normal or not serious.
- Almost 100% of abused women experienced emotional abuse by a partner and the most common perpetrators were parents and other relatives.

(*) FSM National Health and Safety Report. Available at https://pacific.un.org/fsm/refs/fsmsafetyreport.pdf


---
**Page 125**
---

Children of women who experienced partner violence were almost three times more likely to have stopped or dropped out of school than children of women who never experienced partner violence

Some key recommendations for FSM included that national and state-level actions are implemented to:

- Foster mutual social coordination between the high-level and other government agencies (e.g., legislature, judiciary, public safety, social services) and private organizations (e.g., women's groups, NGOs, private health centers) to address VAW in a comprehensive manner
- Create shelters for abused women and children with security staff adequately trained to serve abused women and children. Locate those shelters close by a respected local leader to provide further security to abused women and children from the abusers.
- Develop workshops for parents about parent-child relationships, gender roles, and gender equality
- Promote the passing of the Family Protection Act in Pohnpei, Chuuk, and Yap
- Give special attention to the issue of domestic violence against women, such as the Domestic Violence Unit and the training program for police officers on domestic violence.

# Root Causes

- Socio-cultural and educational access barriers create a lack of socioeconomic empowerment and livelihood opportunity for women and can sometimes limit them to household duties
- Lack of access to credit, finance, technology, and other support services for improved livelihoods and living conditions
- Gender-blind design of options and policy for climate adaptation and development due to exclusion of women from decision making, particularly at the municipal level

# Causes

- Insufficient knowledge of and access to climate information for decision-making, especially at the local level, creates systemic vulnerabilities for communities
- Local communities, particularly women, have heightened vulnerability to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events
- Women-headed households tend to be poorer and occupy more vulnerable land areas, increasing their exposure to climate change impacts and rising sea levels
- Lack of inclusive decision making, stakeholder/beneficiary engagement and priority setting

# Problem Statement

Women in FSM are uniquely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change particularly impacts related to limited disaster risk and recovery capacity, increased food insecurity, and decreased water security as well as the potential to perpetuate/exacerbate existing inequalities.

## Immediate Effects

- Loss of livelihood and life due to increased natural disasters and sea level rise
- Increased number of women reporting mental health issues
- Decreased agricultural productivity and loss of arable land due to climate change impacts, such as increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events and temperature patterns
- Decreased seasonal water availability due to climate impacts
- Loss of assets and infrastructure
- Potential for increased gender-based violence particularly in a DRR setting

## Effects

- Heightened food and water insecurity for vulnerable households with limited means for long-term adaptation strategies and resilience planning
- Loss of livelihoods, particularly for women subsistence farmers, corresponding with an increase in poverty levels among women-headed households
- Exacerbated resiliency planning shortfalls as engagement permanently shifts from men to women
- Deterioration in health and wellbeing, including mental health

# Water Security

## Women and Men in Both Involvement in Managing Water Resources

Women and men are both involved in managing and using water resources, but they have different needs and priorities for these resources. Women are more likely to use and manage water for agriculture and livestock while men are more likely to be responsible for water usage and its management.

## Women's Specific Needs and Priorities

Women face specific challenges related to water security because of their roles in household water collection, preparation, and sanitation. These challenges include the lack of access to clean drinking water, limited time available for water-related tasks, and the need to balance multiple responsibilities within the household. Women also have different needs and priorities when it comes to water resources management.

## Gender-Specific Needs and Priorities

Women's specific needs and priorities are important in developing effective water resource management policies and programs. These include access to clean drinking water, time available for water-related tasks, and the need to balance multiple responsibilities within the household. Women also have different needs and priorities when it comes to water resources management.

## Recommendations

Given the above context for gender, disaster risk and recovery, food security water security, and climate change in FSM, the following recommendations are provided to help ensure effective gender responsive outcomes:

1. **Gender Expertise for Screening and Review of Sub-Grants**: Given the decentralized nature of the sub-grant and limited existing capacity at the LA level, the sub-grant needs to intentionally ensure that gender expertise is included on the review team. If there are no women having staff within the EDA Coordination Unit (ECU) who have gender expertise—the ECU will be charged with screening Expressions of Interest (EOI) as well as reviewing sub-projects to


---
**Page 130**
---

ensure gender has been adequately incorporated into sub-grant design. In addition, the Grants Technical Evaluation Sub-committee should include technical expertise on gender mainstreaming as the body for technical reviews and recommendations. Gender-awareness and particularly an eye towards women’s empowerment must be a key consideration in all aspects of project governance and review structures. Finally, it will also be critical to integrate gender expertise in the planning and implementation phases.

2. Integration of Gender-Responsive Activities into Sub-Grant Design: The EDA Facility will need to ensure that sub-grant design includes specific activities and strategies for inclusive priority setting, implementation modalities, gender mainstreaming, sub-grant gender selection criteria, and sub-grant implementation into inception design discussions, planning, and implementation.

3. Municipal-level Baseline Data: Baseline data disaggregated by gender at the municipal level, particularly in terms of access to resources, assets, and services is critical for understanding the situation on the ground. At this moment, so the EDA Facility has a critical role to play in collecting this information through its stakeholder consultations and other means. The availability of more accurate data will allow for more effective baselines for gender inequality will help to better target interventions going forward and support more informed decision-making.

4. Women’s Groups provide critical inputs to effectively tailoring of sub-grant interventions for example climate resilient techniques and crop varieties to local community contexts, so it is critical for the sub-grants to engage with women’s groups at all levels of project design and implementation through direct consultation and forums to ensure their knowledge can be successfully integrated.

5. Inclusion of Women’s Organizations in Sub-grant Design and Implementation: There is a critical need to include women’s organizations as key partners in the EDA Facility’s work, and create buy-in for addressing the issue of climate change across LAs. These organizations will be critical partners in designing, implementing and sharing the successes and lessons learned of sub-grants.

6. Ensure Women Access Information: The ability to access information and the ways in which information is disseminated are key factors that can either enhance or hinder women’s participation (e.g., through radio, television, print media, community meetings), so in designing dissemination strategies for sub-grants information, the sub-grant will need to ensure that women have an equal pathway to utilizing the available information.

7. Target Barriers Preventing Women from Participation: Women are likely to have additional barriers to participation and access to resources due to diverse and often competing livelihoods (i.e., transportation, social barriers, etc.). During the design of the sub-grants, attention will need to be paid to these issues in order to ensure that women can fully participate in specific interventions.

8. Full Participation of Women in Decision Making: Given the existing issues across FSM with representation in decision-making both at the government and household/community level, the EDA programme will need to proactively work to ensure the full participation of women in decision-making, particularly with regards to sub-project/intervention selection and design at the LA level.
