# Gender Action Plan

## Implementing Roles and Responsibilities for Gender Mainstreaming

The various entities involved in the EDA Facility are all responsible for ensuring gender mainstreaming and the effective execution of the gender action plan, but each has unique and complementary roles and responsibilities.

### EDA Coordination Unit - SPC (through the setting up of an ECU)

- **SFC** is responsible for overall compliance with the GCF gender policy and the monitoring/reporting to GCF.
- SPC also supports the establishment of a Gender Working Group within the EDA Facility's governing body and committee, and will work to ensure that those bodies have gender-balanced representation as well as a gender focal point.
- The ECU will support the EDA Facility in mainstreaming gender considerations into decision-making and review processes, particularly for specific gender criteria used in the reviews of sub-grants. The ECU will also issue tenders for both its Grants Technical Evaluation Sub-contractors and its Gender Mainstreaming Sub-contractors to ensure that they have the required expertise included.
- The ECU also supervises the selection and implementation of priority sub-grants, ensuring that all sub-grant recipients are successfully aligning with the gender action plan and the GCF gender policy.

### FSM Department of Women's Affairs (Nouvelle-Calédonie) - DSWA (through the setting up of an LPC)

- The DSWA is responsible for establishing the EDA Facility's governing bodies and will also assist in the supervision of sub-grant design and implementation. Both of these roles will require close attention to gender considerations, including the need to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are invited to workshops and needs to ensure that this communication is effective at bringing the right people to the table.
- The DSWA will also be responsible for reviewing the EDA Facility's annual review of presented sub-grant proposals submitted by LAs, and will therefore act as a backstop for the integration of gender considerations into the design and implementation of sub-grants.

### SPC Procurement Committee (PC) - The PC is responsible for carrying out evaluations of proposed sub-grant designs and ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are invited to workshops to act efficiently and with due diligence, ensuring that a consistent and equitable method has been used in the evaluation process. The PC will also be responsible for conducting periodic audits of LAs, including gender data and policies and the capacity to integrate gender into sub-grant design.

### Lead Authorities - LAs will coordinate with the ECU and receive support from Facilitating Agents to develop and implement priority sub-grants. This will necessarily include identifying and clarifying gender considerations in the design and implementation of these sub-grants. Additionally, they will be key focal points for the collection of gender-disaggregated data as a result of their work.

### Facilitating Agents - In their work to provide support for sub-grant development and design, Facilitating Agents will be provided training related to gender mainstreaming and provided with specific tools and resources to assist them in this role. The ECU will also provide sub-grant recipients of specific training modules which will include raising awareness on violence against women and girls (VAWG), the importance of gender-disaggregated data collection, grievance redress mechanisms procedures and processes to ensure women's safety and access to GRM is effectively considered and implemented in the design and implementation of the sub-grants.


---
**Page 132**
---

The Facilitating Agents will be providing technical assistance for the design and implementation of the sub-grants, including the specific gender mainstreaming actions as directed by the ECU.

Lastly, Facilitating Agents, as part of their quarterly site visits to sub-grant project sites, will have the opportunity to collect feedback on GBV issues experienced by women beneficiaries, including relating to GBV. Grievances will then be passed on to the ECU.

# Initial Sub-grant Gender Assessment

The sub-grant selection criteria specific to gender will be refined as part of the gender action plan above, but in the framework based on a simplified version of the UN Women template combined with the GCF gender mainstreaming guide is provided in Table below. Given the nature of the sub-grant and limited capacity of the local authorities/designated organizations, it may not always be possible to balance the need for detail to ensure effective gender balanced outcomes that are meeting differentiated needs to all groups of women and men equally. In such cases, the SPC will provide guidance for potential proponents. Sub-grants that can't initially detail specific gender aspects of the sub-grant will receive focused sub-grant support. Ultimately, the local authorities/designated organizations work closely with SPC and the Collective Gender Unit to ensure that all sub-grants are aligned with the gender components and SPC will ensure that they are aligned with the GCF Gender Policy Requirements.

## Table 7: Initial Sub-grant Gender Assessment

| Questioning | Response |
| --- | --- |
| 1. What are the livelihoods of men and women in the area where this sub-grant is to be implemented? | Include any qualitative information, if available. |
| 2. If any, policies or plans related to gender equality exist at the sub-grants local authority? | |
| 3. How does the sub-grant address water security what are the key water security challenges addressed by the sub-grant? How do these differ from those of other sectors? | |
| 4. How is the sub-grant addressing gender considerations for the priority sector identified in Table 5 above? | |
| 5. Based on initial stakeholder engagement, what are the needs and priorities related to the proposed sub-grant activities? Are there any disparities/differences? | |
| 6. Who specifically will be expected to benefit from sub-grant activities? | |
| 7. What specifically will the sub-grant do to ensure women and men have equal opportunity to benefit from these activities? | |
| 8. Are gender outcomes/results gender disaggregated? If no, describe how they will be collected and how they will be monitored |


---
**Page 141**
---

9. What are the biggest risks for achieving gender-responsive outcomes in sub-grant work to overcome these?
10. Who is the authority/designated representative to sign off on the budget for gender in the sub-grant?
11. What funding is allocated for sub-grant gender work/capacity?

# Annex I – Women’s Group Consultations Summary

## Consultation with Pohnpei Women’s Council

December 04, 2020 - Meeting with Pohnpei Women's Council Interim President, Ms. Maria Donne.

The meeting started out with a brief introduction followed by an overview of Pacific Community’s (SPC) Green Climate Fund (GCF) program “Climate change adaptation solutions for Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia.”

The overall EDA programme was discussed as well as key points from the draft GAF.

Key takeaways from the discussion included:

1. Historically this is a matrilineal society where ancestral descent and land is passed down patrilaterally, but now names have changed to last name where most names are mostly passed down from males in some families.
2. We cannot generalize it as strictly matrilinear nor patriarchal because the family’s hereditary agreements are different from one family to another in this modern age.
3. Although there are different roles for males and females at the household level, both males and females have equal rights to land ownership and inheritance.
4. Women still do a lot of the home gardening so that many opportunities are available to women these days that not many are interested in doing home gardening but something else.
5. At his point, what she should consider as a priority project small household farms or gardens that can help supplement other sources of household livelihood or income.

## December 04, 2020 - Meeting with women group representatives from Kosrae, Yap and Chuuk

Conducted a virtual meeting with women group representatives from Kosrae, Yap and Chuuk. Participating in this meeting was the Yap Gender Support Officer representing Yap Women’s groups, President of Yap Women’s Council, and the representative of Kosrae Women’s groups and President if the Chuuk Women's Council representing the Chuuk women’s groups.

The meeting stated out with brief introduction and a brief overview of the SPC’s Green Climate Fund (GCF) program “Climate change adaptation solutions for Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia.” While discussions of the projects outputs and outcomes took place, the representative from Yap lost connection and was unable to participate for the rest of the call.

Prioritization from Kosrae included:

1. Prioritize scaling up food security projects that are ongoing in Kosrae.
2. Local organizations and women groups are in need of capacity building involving adaptation project development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and monitoring.
3. Local government and NGOs to work with state agencies for adaptation projects to prevent project duplication and to collaborate but not compete with each other.


---
**Page 143**
---

# Pacific Community

## Commission des Communautés du Pacifique


### Prioritization for Chuuk: food security projects should be prioritized. Prioritize building upon or scaling up the small-scale security projects that are currently ongoing. She also mentioned that women's groups are also in need of capacity building.

The Chuk representation said she supports the GAAP and stated that she supports the action plans and relevant outputs to land is passed down through material so owning land and property is not an issue in Chuuk. Women do most of the gardening and there’s nothing that will hinder them from participating in the project. She also mentioned that they are very interested in the women’s group and would like to be involved in all the stages of this project to make sure that women's interests are not neglected in designing and selecting priority projects.

### December 07, 2020 - Meeting with Pohnpei Women's Council newly elected officials and several of its members

As the Pohnpei Women’s Council interim president, Mrs. Mona Doron opened the meeting with a brief introduction and introduced the background of the SPC’s Green Climate Fund (GCF) program “Climate change adaptation solutions for Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia”. Windfed went on and presented the outputs of the project and explained in detail the outputs from each of the components including possible activities under each output.

#### Concerns on the outputs

1. Where would the Pohnpei Women’s Council and its members fit in all these since the primary grantee is the principal governments and state agencies?

The national consultant addressed this by explaining that although the executing entity is the State government, the project will be implemented through the principal agencies which will be commissioning their organizational present. They felt that this will be beneficial in developing climate change impact mitigation measures that will have a holistic approach to the communities. The women’s group will be involved in the implementation of the project; however, the activities and projects that they are supporting within their community will be different from what is being implemented by the principal agencies on the project. At the end of the day, vulnerable communities are the primary beneficiaries, and this is where the women's groups can make a difference.

2. Training and capacity building for municipalities and state agencies should prioritize inclusion of females not only as trainers but trainees and mentors as well. How can we make sure that women’s groups will be included in these training sessions? Who would be directly supporting this project? They felt that when it comes to government priority projects, women are often left out or their voices are not heard. This is even more so with training opportunities where often surfaces neglected the inclusion of women or representatives from women's groups.

a. Activities related to this have been included in the Gender Action Plan

The group mentioned that while it is customary that the husband or the father of the household is referred to as the head of the family, in Chuuk, the woman who owns land and property is considered the mother and mother of the household. They have the same rights to property and land as men and it all depends on each family’s situation on who inherits the parcelled land or property. When it comes to cropping, livestock and fishing, women are not exempt and play a critical role in every aspect of their households and their communities.


---
**Page 144**
---

The group mentioned that sometimes what is on paper is not what is actually happening on the ground as times are changing rapidly and most households are trending towards a more modern family structure where both males and females share the same activities, responsibilities and the overall household decisions. They were very interested about the gender action plan and would like to be very much involved in this project moving forward.

When asked what they would consider as priority climate change adaptation projects in relation to the 3 thematic areas:

1. Water security projects that include water tanks and all its associated equipment to deliver water to individual households. Clean running water in all households.
2. Installation of rainwater harvesting systems on municipal water systems
3. Support for women farmer groups

Water security should be a priority for the outer islands as they are currently suffering from extreme drought.

There is not a lot of ideas on priority projects, but they all agreed that their communities could benefit from food and nutrition security projects. They felt that their voices were often left out or were not included during project scoping and capacity building opportunities so they would like to be involved in the GCF projects and capacity building.

There is no reason why the group members would like to be involved in any aspect of this project and they don't foresee anything which could hinder them from participating in the project. Being involved presents an opportunity and not an hindrance from participating in projects that promote community resilience.
